<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the strong isotropic constant conjecture: in each dimension, simplices are the unique maximizers of the isotropic constant among convex bodies. We also prove the sharp entropy bound $`h(f)\ge m+\tfrac12\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}\nolimits (f)`$ for every log-concave probability density on ℝ<sup><i>m</i></sup>, with equality precisely for invertible affine images of products of one-sided exponential laws.

- **claim-simplex-inequality** (theorem, [source](#stmt-intro-simplex)): For every integer $n\ge1$, every convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ satisfies the following bound, with equality if and only if $K$ is a simplex: $$\label{intro:simplex-bound} L_K\le \frac{(n!)^{1/n}}{(n+1)^{(n+1)/(2n)}\sqrt{n+2}}.$$
  - Explanation: Simplices are the unique maximizers of the isotropic constant among convex bodies in every dimension.
- **claim-sharp-entropy-bound** (theorem, [source](#stmt-intro-entropy)): Let $m\ge1$ and let $f$ be a log-concave probability density on $\mathbb R^m$. Then $$\label{intro:entropy-bound} h(f)\ge m+\frac12\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f).$$ Equality holds if and only if $f$ is, almost everywhere, the density of $M(E_1,\ldots,E_m)^{\mathsf T}+b$, where $M$ is an invertible real $m\times m$ matrix, $b\in\mathbb R^m$, and the $E_i$ are independent random variables with density $e^{-u}\mathbf1_{\{u\ge0\}}$.
  - Explanation: The entropy of a log-concave density is bounded below by the dimension plus half the log determinant of its covariance. Equality holds exactly for invertible affine images of products of one-sided exponential laws.
- **claim-q-bijection** (theorem, [source](#stmt-tr-q-properties)): The function $q$ is a smooth increasing bijection from $\mathbb R$ to $(0,\infty)$. Moreover, $$d>0,\qquad 0<q'<1,\qquad q''\ge0, \qquad 0<1-qd\le d^2.$$
  - Explanation: The scalar transport derivative q is a smooth increasing bijection onto the positive reals with derivative bounds, so it can serve as a change of variable.
- **claim-scalar-bounds-basic** (bound, [source](#stmt-tr-scalar-basic)): The functions in <a href="#tr:scalar-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:scalar-functions">[tr:scalar-functions]</a> satisfy, on $\mathbb R$, $$\label{tr:scalar-basic-equations} 0<b<\frac12,\qquad l>0,\qquad \frac{d\log M}{d\log q} :=\frac{M'}{Md}\ge m_0.$$
  - Explanation: Scalar bounds on b and l and a lower bound on the growth of M in log q.
- **claim-affine-normalization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-tr-normalization)): Let $\mu=e^{-V}dy$ satisfy <a href="#tr:smooth-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:smooth-assumptions">[tr:smooth-assumptions]</a>. There exists a positive definite symmetric matrix $P$ such that the Brenier map from $\gamma_m$ to $P_\#\mu$ has Jacobian $J_P$ satisfying $$\mathbb E q^{-1}(J_P)=0.$$ The transformed density again satisfies <a href="#tr:smooth-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:smooth-assumptions">[tr:smooth-assumptions]</a>, with possibly different positive constants.
  - Explanation: An affine change of coordinates makes the reparametrized transport Jacobian field have Gaussian mean zero.
- **claim-jacobian-energy** (theorem, [source](#stmt-tr-energy)): Under <a href="#tr:smooth-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:smooth-assumptions">[tr:smooth-assumptions]</a>, put $J_r=\partial_rJ$ and $$\mathcal L u=\operatorname{tr}(J^{-1}D^2u) -\nabla V(\nabla\phi)\cdot\nabla u.$$ Then $$\label{tr:Jacobian-equation} \mathcal L J_{ij} =-\delta_{ij}+(JD^2V(\nabla\phi)J)_{ij} +\operatorname{tr}(J^{-1}J_iJ^{-1}J_j).$$ Moreover $\\|\partial J\\|<\infty$. The matrix fields $A$ and $k(A)$ belong to Gaussian $H^1$, and the integrations by parts below with smooth matrix functions of $J$ are justified by compact cutoffs.
  - Explanation: The Jacobian satisfies a differentiated transport equation and has finite Gaussian energy, which justifies the integrations by parts.
- **claim-matrix-surplus** (bound, [source](#stmt-tr-surplus)): For the transport satisfying <a href="#tr:smooth-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:smooth-assumptions">[tr:smooth-assumptions]</a>, with the notation above, $$\label{tr:surplus-inequality} \mathbb E\operatorname{tr}k(A)^2 \ge\\|\partial A\\|^2-\\|\partial k(A)\\|^2+\mathcal C.$$
  - Explanation: Convexity gives an energy inequality that keeps a nonnegative eigenvalue-separation surplus.
- **claim-smooth-entropy-remainder** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-slack)): Let $m\ge1$ be an integer and let $\mu=e^{-V}\,\,\mathrm dy$ be a probability measure on $\mathbb R^m$, where $V\in C^\infty(\mathbb R^m)$ and $c_-I\le D^2V\le c_+I$ for constants $0<c_-\le c_+<\infty$. Choose the affine normalization of Proposition <a href="#tr:normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:normalization">5</a>, and let $A=q^{-1}(J)$ be the resulting Gaussian transport matrix field, so $\mathbb EA=0$. Set $$X_r=\mathbb E(x_rA),\qquad Y=A-\sum_{r=1}^m x_rX_r,\qquad B=\sum_{r=1}^mX_r^2.$$ If $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_m$ are the eigenvalues of $B$, then $$\label{mat:strict-remainder} 2h(\mu)-2m-\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(\mu) \ge \frac1{500}\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Y^2) +\frac1{1000}\sum_{i=1}^m\omega(\lambda_i).$$ In particular, $$h(\mu)\ge m+\frac12\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(\mu).$$
  - Explanation: For smooth uniformly convex potentials the entropy inequality holds with an explicit positive remainder controlling the higher chaos part and the first-chaos Gram matrix.
- **claim-covariance-completion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-mat-covariance)): Define the symmetric $H^{-1}$ matrix field $$U=q(A)-(I+N)k(A)$$ and let $W$ be the Gram matrix of its rows in $H^{-1}$: $$W_{ij}=\sum_{\ell=1}^m \langle U_{i\ell},U_{j\ell}\rangle_{-1}.$$ Then $$\label{mat:covariance-completion} C=\mathbb E\bigl[J(RJ)\bigr] =W+\mathbb E\left(2t-k^2-\sum_r K_r^2\right), \qquad K_r=\partial_r k(A).$$ Moreover, if $U=u-\partial^*v$ with $u$ and the family $v=(v_r)_r$ square-integrable, then for every constant $S>0$, $$\label{mat:dual-bound} \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(SW)\le\\|u\\|_S^2+\\|v\\|_S^2.$$
  - Explanation: The covariance matrix equals a dual-norm Gram matrix plus an explicit expectation, with a dual bound on the Gram matrix.
- **claim-matrix-expansion** (bound, [source](#stmt-mat-expansion)): The sum of the last two terms in <a href="#mat:deficit-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:deficit-reduction">[mat:deficit-reduction]</a> is at most $$\begin{aligned} &-\tau(Hp)+\tau\left[T\left(Yh_0+\sum_rX_rM_r\right)\right] \label{mat:expanded-bound}\\ &\quad+\left\\|Y(b-\beta)+\sum_rX_rL_r\right\\|_S^2 +\\|D-\beta g\\|_S^2+\gamma^2\tau\left(T\sum_rg_r^2\right)\nonumber\\ &\quad-P_T-2\tau\left([T,b]\sum_rX_rw_r\right) -\tau\left(T\sum_rw_r^tw_r\right).\nonumber \end{aligned}$$ Except for the use of <a href="#mat:W-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:W-bound">[mat:W-bound]</a>, this expansion is exact.
  - Explanation: An expansion that is exact except for one dual bound isolates the supporting-plane term, the residual squares and the commutator terms.
- **claim-commutator-estimate** (bound, [source](#stmt-mat-commutator)): For the matrices $X_r,B,S,T$ in <a href="#mat:chaos" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:chaos">[mat:chaos]</a> and <a href="#mat:dual-matrices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:dual-matrices">[mat:dual-matrices]</a>, any square-integrable symmetric matrix field $b$, and arbitrary square-integrable matrix fields $w_r$, $$\label{mat:commutator-bound} -P_T-2\tau\left([T,b]\sum_rX_rw_r\right) -\tau\left(T\sum_rw_r^tw_r\right) \le\left(1+\frac s8\right)\\|w\\|^2.$$
  - Explanation: The compensating quadratic term controls the noncommuting factors, giving a dimension-free bound.
- **claim-first-chaos-absorption** (bound, [source](#stmt-mat-scalar-absorption)): Let $A\in L^2(\gamma_m;\operatorname{Sym}_m)$ satisfy $\mathbb EA=0$, and define $X_r,Y,B$ by <a href="#mat:chaos" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:chaos">[mat:chaos]</a> and $S,T,H$ by <a href="#mat:dual-matrices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:dual-matrices">[mat:dual-matrices]</a>. With the constants <a href="#mat:dual-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:dual-constants">[mat:dual-constants]</a>, <a href="#mat:constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:constants">[mat:constants]</a>, and <a href="#mat:completion-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:completion-constants">[mat:completion-constants]</a>, the functions <a href="#mat:scalar-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:scalar-functions">[mat:scalar-functions]</a>, and $\rho=\epsilon I+\kappa S/s$, one has $$\label{mat:absorption-inequality} \begin{split} &\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(T-\log S)-\tau(H\rho p) +s\tau\left[Y^2\left((b-\beta)^2+ \frac{\alpha h_0^2}{4(1-\epsilon-\kappa)p}\right)\right]\\ &\hspace{15mm}\le\frac{69}{500}\\|Y\\|^2 -\frac1{1000}\sum_{i=1}^m\omega(\lambda_i), \end{split}$$ where $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_m$ are the eigenvalues of $B$. All scalar factors in the expected traces are evaluated at $A$.
  - Explanation: The cost of the supporting plane and the terms quadratic in the higher chaos are absorbed, leaving a negative remainder in the eigenvalues of the first-chaos Gram matrix.
- **claim-two-eigenvalue-estimate** (bound, [source](#stmt-mat-scalar-pair)): Let $a_i,a_j\in\mathbb R$ and $G,g,Z\in\mathbb R$. Set $$\delta=\bigl\|\log(q(a_i)/q(a_j))\bigr\|,\qquad r_{ij}=\frac{1+m_0(\min(a_i,a_j))}{16} \left(1+\frac{\delta^2}{30}+\frac{\delta^4}{1680}\right),$$ where $m_0$ is the six-interval lower bound in <a href="#tr:bins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:bins">[tr:bins]</a>. Define the endpoint residuals $$\begin{array}{lll} D_j=Gb_j+Z\Delta k_{ij},&L_j=Gl_j+Z\Delta b_{ij},& M_j=Ge_j+Z\psi_{ij}+cgb_j,\\ D_i=Gb_i+Z\Delta k_{ji},&L_i=Gl_i+Z\Delta b_{ji},& M_i=Ge_i+Z\psi_{ji}+cgb_i. \end{array}$$ Let $Q(u,v)=\frac{43}{100}u^2+\frac{26}{100}uv+\frac{68}{25}v^2$. Then $$\begin{aligned} &\operatorname{avg}_{v=i,j}\left[ s(D_v-\beta g)^2+\left(1+\frac s8\right)(D_v-\gamma g)^2 +\gamma^2(s-1)g^2+\frac{L_v^2}{\alpha} +\frac{M_v^2}{4(1-\epsilon)p_v}\right]\notag\\ &\hspace{15mm}\le Q(G,g-G/2)+r_{ij}\delta^2Z^2. \label{mat:pair-inequality} \end{aligned}$$ Here $\operatorname{avg}_{v=i,j}$ is the arithmetic mean of the two endpoint expressions, counting both also when $i=j$. Moreover $p_v=p(a_v)>0$, and all divided differences extend continuously to $a_i=a_j$.
  - Explanation: A scalar quadratic estimate for a pair of eigenvalues controls the derivative residuals while retaining the separation surplus.
- **claim-one-variable-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-sc-bounds)): For every $a\in\mathbb R$, $$\label{sc:p-h-bounds} \begin{gathered} p(a)>0,\qquad \|h_0(a)\|\le .67\sqrt{p(a)},\\ p(a)\ge\begin{cases}(7+a^2)^{-1},&a\le0,\\ .25,&a\ge0,\end{cases} \qquad p(a)\ge .46\quad(a\ge4). \end{gathered}$$ The following interval bounds hold: $$\label{sc:interval-table} \begin{array}{c\|cccccc} &b&\mathsf L\le&\mathsf D\le&K_0&J_0\le&\ell_1\\\hline O &[0,.09]&.60&.295(7+a^2)&[.37,.56]&.72&.13\\ P_1 &[.083,.15]&1.01&4.3&[.29,.46]&.72&.13\\ R_1 &[.14,.23]&1.19&3.00&[.29,.371]&.68&.13\\ U_1 &[.22,.342]&1.19&1.72&[.29,.398]&.61&.123\\ V_1 &[.337,.45]&.83&.87&[.31,.4]&.50&.060\\ T_1 &[.445,.5]&.20&.640&[.32,.4]&.48&.022 \end{array}$$ Here $\ell_1$ is the positive number in the last column, and $$\label{sc:local-surplus} Q- \begin{pmatrix} A_*b^2-\eta_*b+\nu_*/4+l^2/\alpha&\nu_*/2-\eta_*b\\ \nu_*/2-\eta_*b&\nu_* \end{pmatrix} -\mathsf D \begin{pmatrix}\mathsf E\\cb\end{pmatrix} \begin{pmatrix}\mathsf E&cb\end{pmatrix} \succeq\begin{pmatrix}\ell_1&0\\0&.75\end{pmatrix}.$$ Here $Q=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}.43&.13\\.13&2.72\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, as in Proposition <a href="#mat:scalar-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:scalar-pair">13</a>. Finally, writing $$\label{sc:derivative-functions} z=\frac{l'}{l},\qquad h_*=1+3b+kz,\qquad \chi=\frac{l}{d},$$ we have $$\label{sc:derivative-table} \begin{array}{c\|ccc} &z&h_*&\chi\le\\\hline a\le-1&[-.08,.70]&[.8,1.77]&.070\\ a\ge-1&[-.475,0]&[.8,1.77]&.097 \end{array}$$ At an endpoint shared by two intervals, either applicable row may be used.
  - Explanation: Rational one-variable bounds on the scalar functions, certified on all of the real line, feed both scalar propositions.
- **claim-linear-coercivity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geo-coercivity)): Let $V:\mathbb R^m\to(-\infty,+\infty]$ be proper, lower semicontinuous, and convex, with $$0<\int_{\mathbb R^m}e^{-V(x)}\,\,\mathrm dx<\infty.$$ Then there are $a>0$ and $b\geq0$ such that $$\label{geo:linear-coercivity} V(x)\geq a\|x\|-b\qquad(x\in\mathbb R^m).$$ In particular, $e^{-V}$ has moments of every order, and $\int \|V\|e^{-V}<\infty$, where $\|V\|e^{-V}$ is interpreted as zero when $V=+\infty$.
  - Explanation: An integrable proper lower semicontinuous convex potential grows at least linearly, so the density has all moments and finite entropy integrand.
- **claim-smooth-approximation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-geo-approximation)): Let $f=e^{-V}$ be a log-concave probability density on $\mathbb R^m$, represented by a proper lower semicontinuous convex potential $V$. There are smooth convex potentials $V_j$ and probability densities $$f_j=Z_j^{-1}e^{-V_j},\qquad Z_j=\int_{\mathbb R^m}e^{-V_j},$$ such that $$\label{geo:hessian-bounds} \frac2jI\preceq D^2V_j\preceq\left(j+\frac2j\right)I \qquad(j\geq1),$$ and $$\label{geo:approximation-limits} Z_j\longrightarrow1,\qquad \lVert f_j-f\rVert_{L^1}\longrightarrow0,\qquad h(f_j)\longrightarrow h(f),\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f_j)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f).$$ The covariance limit is entrywise, the means of $f_j$ converge to the mean of $f$, and all these quantities are finite.
  - Explanation: A log-concave density is approximated by smooth densities with bounded positive Hessians, preserving entropy, covariance and mean in the limit.
- **claim-linear-field-rigidity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-linear-jacobian)): Let $m\ge1$, and let $X_1,\ldots,X_m$ be real symmetric $m\times m$ matrices satisfying $$\sum_{r=1}^m X_r^2=I, \qquad A(x)=\sum_{r=1}^m x_rX_r.$$ Suppose $F\in W^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^m;\mathbb R^m)$ has weak Jacobian $DF=q(A)$ almost everywhere. Then there are an orthogonal matrix $U$, signs $\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_m\in\{-1,1\}$, and $w\in\mathbb R^m$ such that $$\label{eq:rigid-map} U^{\mathsf T}F(Uy) =\bigl(\sigma_i t(\sigma_i y_i)\bigr)_{i=1}^m+w \qquad\text{for almost every }y\in\mathbb R^m.$$ In particular, $F_\#\gamma_m$ is an affine image of the product of $m$ mean-one exponential laws, and its covariance matrix is $I$.
  - Explanation: If the transform q of a linear symmetric field is a weak Jacobian, the coefficient matrices commute and the map is a rotated product of scalar exponential transports.
- **claim-spectral-lipschitz** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz)): For real symmetric matrices $M,N\in\mathbb R^{m\times m}$, $$\label{eq:spectral-lipschitz} \|q(M)-q(N)\|_{\mathrm F}\le \|M-N\|_{\mathrm F}.$$
  - Explanation: The matrix function induced by q is Lipschitz with constant one in Frobenius norm.

## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Gaussian distribution function and density** ([source](#tr:scalar-functions)): Let $\Phi$ and $\varphi$ denote the distribution function and density of a standard one-dimensional Gaussian.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-q-properties** [claim](#stmt-tr-q-properties) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tr-q-properties): Gaussian integration by parts, hazard-rate submultiplicativity -> q is a smooth increasing bijection with derivative bounds
- **step-scalar-certificate** [claim](#stmt-tr-scalar-basic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz-4): Tail asymptotics plus rational polynomial enclosures with positivity certificates -> scalar bounds on b, l and the log-growth of M
- **step-normalization** [claim](#stmt-tr-normalization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tr-normalization): Homotopy from the Gaussian, compactness of zeros, Brouwer degree -> affine normalization with mean-zero reparametrized Jacobian
- **step-energy** [claim](#stmt-tr-energy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tr-energy): Differentiate the density equation, divergence form, cutoffs and Fatou -> Jacobian equation and finite Gaussian energy
- **step-surplus** [claim](#stmt-tr-surplus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-tr-surplus): Eigenbasis rotation, rearrangement, logarithmic Chebyshev comparison -> matrix surplus inequality
- **step-covariance** [claim](#stmt-mat-covariance) / [proof](#proof-stmt-mat-covariance): Chaos expansion and dual-norm pairing -> covariance completion and dual bound
- **step-expansion** [claim](#stmt-mat-expansion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-mat-expansion): Exact algebraic expansion with divided-difference residuals -> expanded bound for the deficit terms
- **step-commutator** [claim](#stmt-mat-commutator) / [proof](#proof-stmt-mat-commutator): Diagonalize S, complete the square, bound a two-by-two quadratic form -> commutator estimate
- **step-absorption** [claim](#stmt-mat-scalar-absorption) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sc-bounds): Quadratic minorant of p against spectral moments and a logarithmic bound -> first-chaos absorption with negative remainder
- **step-pair-estimate** [claim](#stmt-mat-scalar-pair) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sc-bounds-2): Endpoint quadratic form and comparison with the separation surplus -> two-eigenvalue estimate
- **step-one-variable-bounds** [claim](#stmt-sc-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz-4): Negative tail, positive tail and central interval certificates -> interval tables for scalar functions
- **step-smooth-entropy** [claim](#stmt-prop-slack) / [proof](#proof-stmt-mat-scalar-pair): Combine expansion, commutator, absorption and pair estimates, then sum over chaos degrees -> smooth entropy bound with remainder
- **step-coercivity** [claim](#stmt-geo-coercivity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-coercivity): Sublevel ball, bounded convex set, convexity along rays -> linear growth of the potential
- **step-approximation** [claim](#stmt-geo-approximation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-approximation): Moreau envelope, mollification, dominated convergence -> smooth approximants converging in entropy and covariance
- **step-entropy-inequality** [claim](#stmt-intro-entropy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-geo-approximation-2): Apply the smooth bound to approximants and pass to the limit -> entropy inequality for all log-concave densities
- **step-spectral-lipschitz** [claim](#stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz): Entrywise comparison in two eigenbases -> Frobenius Lipschitz bound for q
- **step-linear-rigidity** [claim](#stmt-lem-linear-jacobian) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-linear-jacobian): Power series of q at zero, compatibility of derivatives, simultaneous diagonalization -> commuting coefficients and exponential product structure
- **step-simplex-inequality** [claim](#stmt-intro-simplex) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz-3): Exponential density on the cone over the body, entropy inequality in one higher dimension, support analysis for equality -> sharp isotropic constant bound with simplex equality

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-simplex-cone-equality**: For a simplex K in dimension n, the cone inequality (n!)^2\|K\|^2 >= (n+1)^{n+1}(n+2)^n det(Sigma_K) holds with equality, because the covariance determinant of the simplex makes both sides coincide.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-nonconvex-set**: Apply the simplex bound on the isotropic constant to a set that is not a convex body, such as a finite lattice subset. -> The bound and its equality case are only asserted for convex bodies, so no conclusion about other sets follows.
- **anti-non-logconcave-entropy**: Use the sharp entropy lower bound for a density that is not log-concave. -> The inequality is only stated for log-concave densities, so it may fail otherwise.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The equality case of the cone computation for a simplex is a finite exact calculation: covariance matrix of the standard simplex, its determinant, and the two sides of the cone inequality. The general inequality itself is analytic and is not checked here.

```python
# INPUTS: one integer n >= 1 on stdin (dimension of the standard simplex conv{0,e_1,...,e_n}).
# PRECONDITIONS: n is a positive integer, small enough for exact rational arithmetic.
# OUTPUTS: one line 'lhs rhs' with exact fractions: (n!)^2|K|^2 and (n+1)^(n+1)(n+2)^n det(Sigma_K).
# TIME: O(n^3) fraction operations plus big-integer powers.
# SPACE: O(n^2) fractions.
# ARITHMETIC: exact rationals (fractions.Fraction), no floating point.
# FAILURE: non-integer or non-positive input raises an error and a nonzero exit.
import sys
from fractions import Fraction
from math import factorial


def det(M):
    k = len(M)
    A = [row[:] for row in M]
    r = Fraction(1)
    for c in range(k):
        p = None
        for i in range(c, k):
            if A[i][c] != 0:
                p = i
                break
        if p is None:
            return Fraction(0)
        if p != c:
            A[c], A[p] = A[p], A[c]
            r = -r
        r *= A[c][c]
        for i in range(c + 1, k):
            f = A[i][c] / A[c][c]
            for j in range(c, k):
                A[i][j] -= f * A[c][j]
    return r


n = int(sys.stdin.read().split()[0])
if n < 1:
    raise ValueError('n must be positive')
d = Fraction(1, (n + 1) * (n + 2))
S = [[d * ((1 if i == j else 0) - Fraction(1, n + 1)) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]
vol = Fraction(1, factorial(n))
lhs = Fraction(factorial(n) ** 2) * vol * vol
rhs = (n + 1) ** (n + 1) * (n + 2) ** n * det(S)
print(lhs, rhs)
```

    "language": "python",
    "mutants": [
      {
        "expected_failure": "Covariance and determinant differ, so the reported right side disagrees with the oracle.",
        "id": "mut-wrong-centering",
        "mutation": "Replace the centering factor 1/(n+1) in the covariance by 1/(n+2).",
        "observed_exit": 1,
        "receipt_sha256": "2cb386f0f7e275aaa7a796ac68d3aeb521c39fd3043ea7f5fbc96c6dc78455ee"
      },
      {
        "expected_failure": "Right side differs from the oracle value for every n.",
        "id": "mut-wrong-exponent",
        "mutation": "Lower the exponent of (n+2) in the right side by one.",
        "observed_exit": 1,
        "receipt_sha256": "c99207f518b1ef5c1d0fdc5bc437d04158dd6ac8ecd6e3a1b14febdbc7c2e98a"
      },
      {
        "expected_failure": "Determinant is wrong for n >= 2 and the oracle rejects.",
        "id": "mut-det-product",
        "mutation": "Overwrite the running determinant instead of multiplying by the pivot.",
        "observed_exit": 1,
        "receipt_sha256": "bb9da765d7403b704fa3fa0a17c77943edc8c2f5a5e568f553ac67a774414347"
      },
      {
        "expected_failure": "Elimination no longer zeroes the column, giving a wrong determinant for n >= 2.",
        "id": "mut-elimination-sign",
        "mutation": "Add instead of subtract the multiple of the pivot row during elimination.",
        "observed_exit": 1,
        "receipt_sha256": "2974cae03c5138b2ac18bdacce071b456d4e5cbdc2388f9f7b8d3e2ec29c7948"
      }
    ],
    "oracle_code": "import sys\nimport itertools\nfrom fractions import Fraction\nfrom math import factorial\n\n\ndef perm_det(M):\n    k = len(M)\n    tot = Fraction(0)\n    for p in itertools.permutations(range(k)):\n        s = 1\n        for a in range(k):\n            for b in range(a + 1, k):\n                if p[a] > p[b]:\n                    s = -s\n        t = Fraction(s)\n        for i in range(k):\n            t *= M[i][p[i]]\n        tot += t\n    return tot\n\n\ndef mom(a):\n    n = len(a)\n    num = 1\n    for e in a:\n        num *= factorial(e)\n    return Fraction(num * factorial(n), factorial(n + sum(a)))\n\n\ndef unit(n, *idx):\n    v = [0] * n\n    for i in idx:\n        v[i] += 1\n    return v\n\n\ndef main():\n    n = int(open(sys.argv[1]).read().split()[0])\n    toks = open(sys.argv[2]).read().split()\n    expected = sys.argv[3].strip()\n    if len(toks) != 2:\n        sys.exit(1)\n    try:\n        cl, cr = Fraction(toks[0]), Fraction(toks[1])\n    except Exception:\n        sys.exit(1)\n    mean = [mom(unit(n, i)) for i in range(n)]\n    cov = [[mom(unit(n, i, j)) - mean[i] * mean[j] for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]\n    vol = Fraction(1, factorial(n))\n    lhs = Fraction(factorial(n) ** 2) * vol * vol\n    rhs = (n + 1) ** (n + 1) * (n + 2) ** n * perm_det(cov)\n    if (cl, cr) != (lhs, rhs):\n        sys.exit(1)\n    if (lhs == rhs) != (expected == 'EQ'):\n        sys.exit(1)\n    sys.exit(0)\n\n\nmain()\n",
    "output_contract": "stdout: two exact fractions 'lhs rhs'; expected token EQ means lhs equals rhs, NE means they differ.",
    "reason": "The equality case of the cone computation for a simplex is a finite exact calculation: covariance matrix of the standard simplex, its determinant, and the two sides of the cone inequality. The general inequality itself is analytic and is not checked here.",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: one integer n >= 1 on stdin (dimension of the standard simplex conv{0,e_1,...,e_n}).\n# PRECONDITIONS: n is a positive integer, small enough for exact rational arithmetic.\n# OUTPUTS: one line 'lhs rhs' with exact fractions: (n!)^2|K|^2 and (n+1)^(n+1)(n+2)^n det(Sigma_K).\n# TIME: O(n^3) fraction operations plus big-integer powers.\n# SPACE: O(n^2) fractions.\n# ARITHMETIC: exact rationals (fractions.Fraction), no floating point.\n# FAILURE: non-integer or non-positive input raises an error and a nonzero exit.\nimport sys\nfrom fractions import Fraction\nfrom math import factorial\n\n\ndef det(M):\n    k = len(M)\n    A = [row[:] for row in M]\n    r = Fraction(1)\n    for c in range(k):\n        p = None\n        for i in range(c, k):\n            if A[i][c] != 0:\n                p = i\n                break\n        if p is None:\n            return Fraction(0)\n        if p != c:\n            A[c], A[p] = A[p], A[c]\n            r = -r\n        r *= A[c][c]\n        for i in range(c + 1, k):\n            f = A[i][c] / A[c][c]\n            for j in range(c, k):\n                A[i][j] -= f * A[c][j]\n    return r\n\n\nn = int(sys.stdin.read().split()[0])\nif n < 1:\n    raise ValueError('n must be positive')\nd = Fraction(1, (n + 1) * (n + 2))\nS = [[d * ((1 if i == j else 0) - Fraction(1, n + 1)) for j in range(n)] for i in range(n)]\nvol = Fraction(1, factorial(n))\nlhs = Fraction(factorial(n) ** 2) * vol * vol\nrhs = (n + 1) ** (n + 1) * (n + 2) ** n * det(S)\nprint(lhs, rhs)\n",

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is a sharp inequality with an equality classification. It gives no procedure with explicit steps that a program could execute. The only certified computation is the scalar bound verification, which supports the proof and is not a deliverable. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The statements concern extremal values of a volume-covariance quantity for convex bodies. They give no dynamics, sampling scheme or simulation rule. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No tool is specified. The transport and normalization steps are existence arguments through Brenier maps, topological degree and compactness, so they are not usable as an interface. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The proof is not constructive as a finite procedure. It uses an optimal transport map, a homotopy with a degree argument, and limits of approximating densities, so no algorithm with a stated cost follows. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No data structure is defined. The objects are measures, matrix fields and Gaussian chaos components, which are continuous analytic objects. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no fixed-size numeric kernel. The finite polynomial certificates in the appendix are proof verification and are not a workload designed for accelerators. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardness assumption, adversary model or security notion appears. The entropy bound concerns continuous differential entropy of log-concave densities. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The entropy bound holds for log-concave densities with positive definite covariance. It is not a learning guarantee. Using it for a fitted density would also require the log-concavity hypothesis to hold. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper is about extremal bodies and equality cases, not about an optimisation problem with a solver. The Brenier map is an existence tool in the proof. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There are no nodes, messages, faults or consensus. Nothing in the statements concerns coordination of processes. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The natural home is pure mathematics: convex geometry, the nonsymmetric volume product through a known cone reduction, and entropy inequalities. That use is theoretical and gives no implementable contract here. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-convex-body-hypothesis** (hypothesis): The simplex bound and its equality classification are stated only for convex bodies, with the dimension n at least one. Nothing is asserted for sets that are not convex bodies. Blocked use: ['Applying the simplex bound to a non-convex set or to a finite lattice subset', 'Reading the equality statement as a classification of near-maximizers']
- **limit-logconcave-density** (hypothesis): The sharp entropy bound is proved for log-concave probability densities on a Euclidean space. Its equality classification is limited to that class, so the inequality is not asserted for other densities. Blocked use: ['Applying the entropy lower bound to a mixture or any other non-log-concave density', 'Using the differential entropy bound for a discrete distribution']
- **limit-smooth-regime** (formal_scope): The strict remainder estimate is proved only for smooth potentials whose Hessian lies between two positive multiples of the identity. The general theorem is reached afterwards by approximation, so the explicit remainder is not claimed for the original density. Blocked use: ['Quoting the remainder coefficients for a non-smooth or non-uniformly convex density', 'Treating the constants of the smooth estimate as bounds for the limiting density']
- **limit-normalization-existence** (nonconstructive): The affine normalization that makes the reparametrized Jacobian have Gaussian mean zero is obtained from a homotopy and a degree argument. This gives existence of the normalizing matrix and no procedure to compute it. Blocked use: ['Implementing the normalization as an algorithm with a guaranteed cost', 'Assuming that the normalizing matrix is unique or explicitly computable']
- **limit-approximation-convergence** (approximation): The approximating densities converge to the target only in the limit. Entropy, covariance and mean are preserved in the limit, and no finite-stage error rate is stated. Blocked use: ['Using a single approximant as an error-controlled substitute for the target density', 'Quoting a rate of convergence for the entropy or covariance']
- **limit-positive-definite-covariance** (edge_case): The covariance determinant is meaningful because a Lebesgue density cannot be supported on a hyperplane. This uses full-dimensional support, so a degenerate distribution falls outside the stated range. Blocked use: ['Applying the entropy bound to a distribution concentrated on a lower-dimensional subspace', 'Taking the logarithm of a singular covariance determinant']
- **limit-exact-rational-certificate** (formal_scope): The scalar bounds rest on finite certificates in exact rational arithmetic. They concern the specific scalar functions and constants of the paper, and a change of constants requires a new certificate. Blocked use: ['Reusing the tabulated bounds with different constants without rechecking them', 'Replacing the certificate by numerical sampling']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the strong isotropic constant conjecture: in each dimension, simplices are the unique maximizers of the isotropic constant among convex bodies. We also prove the sharp entropy bound $`h(f)\ge m+\tfrac12\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}\nolimits (f)`$ for every log-concave probability density on ℝ<sup><i>m</i></sup>, with equality precisely for invertible affine images of products of one-sided exponential laws.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

The isotropic constant compares the covariance of a uniform distribution with the volume of its support. For a convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$, meaning a compact convex set with nonempty interior, write $|K|$ for its Lebesgue volume, $b_K=|K|^{-1}\int_Kx\,\,\mathrm dx$ for its centroid, and $$\Sigma_K=\frac1{|K|}\int_K(x-b_K)(x-b_K)^{\mathsf T}\,\,\mathrm dx.$$ Its isotropic constant is <a id="intro:isotropic-constant"></a>$$\label{intro:isotropic-constant}
 L_K=\left(\frac{\det\Sigma_K}{|K|^2}\right)^{1/(2n)}.$$ This quantity is unchanged by invertible affine maps. The slicing problem, arising in Bourgain’s work on convex bodies (Bourgain 1986) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Bourgain1986](#ref-Bourgain1986)], asks whether it admits an upper bound independent of dimension. Klartag proved an $O(n^{1/4})$ bound (Bo’az Klartag 2006) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Klartag2006](#ref-Klartag2006)]. Subsequent progress used stochastic localization, introduced by Eldan (Eldan 2013) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Eldan2013](#ref-Eldan2013)], leading through subpolynomial and polylogarithmic estimates (Chen 2021; Bo’az Klartag and Lehec 2022; Bo’az Klartag 2023) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Chen2021](#ref-Chen2021), [KlartagLehec2022](#ref-KlartagLehec2022), [Klartag2023](#ref-Klartag2023)] to Klartag and Lehec’s dimension-independent bound using Guan’s covariance estimate (Guan 2024; Boaz Klartag and Lehec 2025) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Guan2024](#ref-Guan2024), [KlartagLehec2025](#ref-KlartagLehec2025)]. The sharp form, also called the strong isotropic constant conjecture, asks, in each dimension, whether a simplex maximizes $L_K$. The distinction is substantial: a universal bound does not identify the extremal value or an extremizing body.

In the plane, Campi, Colesanti, and Gronchi proved the simplex bound, and Saroglou established uniqueness of the maximizing body (Campi, Colesanti, and Gronchi 1999; Saroglou 2010) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[CampiColesantiGronchi1999](#ref-CampiColesantiGronchi1999), [Saroglou2010](#ref-Saroglou2010)]. In higher dimensions, geometric variation arguments have imposed restrictions on possible maximizers. Rademacher proved that a simplicial polytope maximizing the isotropic constant among all convex bodies must be a simplex (Rademacher 2016) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Rademacher2016](#ref-Rademacher2016)]. More recent restrictions on local maximizers in terms of decomposability appear in (Kipp 2026) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Kipp2026](#ref-Kipp2026)]. We prove the sharp inequality for arbitrary convex bodies and classify all equality cases.

<a id="stmt-intro-simplex"></a>
<div id="intro:simplex" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1** (The simplex inequality). *For every integer $n\ge1$, every convex body $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ satisfies the following bound, with equality if and only if $K$ is a simplex: <a id="intro:simplex-bound"></a>$$\label{intro:simplex-bound}
 L_K\le
 \frac{(n!)^{1/n}}{(n+1)^{(n+1)/(2n)}\sqrt{n+2}}.$$*

</div>

The theorem also has a volume-product consequence. For a convex body $K$ with $0$ in its interior, define its polar by $K^\circ=\{y\in\mathbb R^n:\langle x,y\rangle\le1\text{ for every }x\in K\}$. Using exponential measures on convex cones, Klartag proved that the sharp simplex bound implies <a id="intro:mahler"></a>$$\label{intro:mahler}
 |K|\,|K^\circ|\ge\frac{(n+1)^{n+1}}{(n!)^2},$$ the nonsymmetric Mahler inequality (Bo’az Klartag 2018, Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Klartag2018](#ref-Klartag2018)]. Thus Theorem <a href="#intro:simplex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:simplex">1</a> also gives this inequality in every dimension.

The proof proceeds through differential entropy. For a probability density $f$ on $\mathbb R^m$, define, when the integrals are finite, $$h(f)=-\int_{\mathbb R^m}f(x)\log f(x)\,\,\mathrm dx,
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f)=\int_{\mathbb R^m}(x-\bar x)(x-\bar x)^{\mathsf T}f(x)\,\,\mathrm dx,
 \quad \bar x=\int_{\mathbb R^m}xf(x)\,\,\mathrm dx.$$ All logarithms are natural, and $0\log0=0$. A log-concave density can be represented, up to a null set, as $f=e^{-V}$ for a proper lower semicontinuous convex function $V:\mathbb R^m\to(-\infty,+\infty]$. The value $+\infty$ permits a density to vanish. Such a probability density has finite entropy and moments, and its covariance is positive definite; the needed tail bound is proved in Lemma <a href="#geo:coercivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:coercivity">15</a>.

<a id="stmt-intro-entropy"></a>
<div id="intro:entropy" class="theorem">

<a id="intro:entropy-bound"></a>
**Theorem 2** (The sharp entropy bound). *Let $m\ge1$ and let $f$ be a log-concave probability density on $\mathbb R^m$. Then $$\label{intro:entropy-bound}
 h(f)\ge m+\frac12\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f).$$ Equality holds if and only if $f$ is, almost everywhere, the density of $M(E_1,\ldots,E_m)^{\mathsf T}+b$, where $M$ is an invertible real $m\times m$ matrix, $b\in\mathbb R^m$, and the $E_i$ are independent random variables with density $e^{-u}\mathbf1_{\{u\ge0\}}$.*

</div>

Sharpness follows from a product of $m$ independent exponential variables of mean one: its entropy is $m$ and its covariance is the identity. Both sides of <a href="#intro:entropy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:entropy-bound">[intro:entropy-bound]</a> increase by $\log|\det A|$ under an invertible affine change $x\mapsto Ax+b$. Thus the theorem asserts that a log-concave density with identity covariance has entropy at least $m$.

Bobkov and Madiman developed the relation between entropy bounds for log-concave measures and the slicing problem (Bobkov and Madiman 2011) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BobkovMadiman2011](#ref-BobkovMadiman2011)]. Fradelizi and Marín Sola formulated the sharp entropy statement <a href="#intro:entropy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:entropy-bound">[intro:entropy-bound]</a> and proved its equivalence, across dimensions, with the simplex bound (Fradelizi and Marín Sola 2026, Conjecture 3(iii) and Proposition 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[FradeliziMarinSola2026](#ref-FradeliziMarinSola2026)]. In dimension one, Melbourne, Nayar, and Roberto proved the sharp bound, attained by a one-sided exponential distribution (Melbourne, Nayar, and Roberto 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[MelbourneNayarRoberto2026](#ref-MelbourneNayarRoberto2026)]. Theorem <a href="#intro:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:entropy">2</a> establishes the inequality and identifies all equality cases in every dimension. The cone reduction used here follows the preceding geometric and entropic work; we include it both to derive the dimensional constant and to transfer the equality classification to convex bodies.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Ideas of the proof

We first assume that $f=e^{-V}$ is smooth and that the Hessian of $V$ is bounded above and below by positive multiples of the identity. Let $\nabla\phi$ transport standard Gaussian measure to $f$, and put $J=D^2\phi$. Brenier’s theorem and Caffarelli’s regularity and contraction theory supply this positive definite matrix field and its uniform bounds (Brenier 1991; McCann 1995; Caffarelli 2000; Cordero-Erausquin and Figalli 2019) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Brenier1991](#ref-Brenier1991), [McCann1995](#ref-McCann1995), [Caffarelli2000](#ref-Caffarelli2000), [CorderoErausquinFigalli2019](#ref-CorderoErausquinFigalli2019)]. The scalar model is the transport from a Gaussian variable to a mean-one exponential variable, $$t(a)=-\log\Phi(-a),\qquad q(a)=t'(a),$$ where $\Phi$ is the standard Gaussian distribution function. Since $q$ is an increasing bijection onto $(0,\infty)$, spectral calculus defines the symmetric matrix field $A=q^{-1}(J)$.

Two features of this parametrization drive the proof. First, an affine change of the target can be chosen so that $\mathbb EA=0$, where expectation is Gaussian. This nonlinear normalization is proved by a homotopy and a compactness argument; ordinary covariance normalization does not give the required identity. Second, differentiating the transport equation and using convexity of $V$ gives an energy estimate for $A$. The estimate retains a nonnegative term involving pairs of distinct eigenvalues of $A$. Discarding that term would lose the control needed for matrix functions whose derivatives do not commute.

The zero mean permits an orthogonal decomposition $$A(x)=\sum_{r=1}^m x_rX_r+Y(x),
 \qquad X_r=\mathbb E[x_rA(x)],$$ where $Y$ has Gaussian chaos degrees at least two. The first term lies in the equality space of the Gaussian Poincaré inequality and hence has no energy surplus. We instead use its constant matrices $X_r$ to choose a supporting plane for the covariance log determinant. The higher-degree part supplies a strict spectral gap. A covariance identity involving the resolvent of the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck operator then expresses the signed deficit $m+\tfrac12\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f)-h(f)$ in terms of these two parts.

The resulting estimate has both noncommuting matrix terms and scalar divided differences. A commutator square controls the former. One scalar inequality absorbs the cost of the supporting plane and the terms without derivatives. A second controls the divided differences, charging their off-diagonal error to the eigenvalue-separation term retained earlier. After this cancellation, the entropy deficit is bounded above by a quadratic expression that is nonpositive on every chaos of degree at least two. Retaining strict slack also controls $Y$ and forces $\sum_rX_r^2$ towards the identity when the entropy gap tends to zero. All numerical constants in the scalar inequalities are exact rationals. Their verification uses polynomial bounds on a compact interval and separate analytic estimates on both tails.

For equality, it is essential that this remainder applies to nearly sharp densities. The expected extremizers have restricted support and affine potentials, so equality cannot be studied solely within the smooth, uniformly convex class. We approximate an arbitrary equality density while preserving entropy and covariance. The remainder then forces the reparametrized Jacobians to converge in Gaussian $L^2$ to a linear field $A(x)=\sum_rx_rX_r$, with $\sum_rX_r^2=I$. Since $q$ is Lipschitz in Frobenius norm, the original Jacobians converge to $q(A)$, and Gaussian Poincaré gives convergence of the centered maps.

The local compatibility of this limiting Jacobian is decisive. The constant and linear terms of its mixed-derivative identities force the symmetric matrices $X_r$ to commute. Simultaneous orthogonal diagonalization then gives independent one-dimensional exponential transports. Only the nonvanishing of $q'(0)$ and $q''(0)$ is needed for the commutation argument, isolating a potentially reusable algebraic step. No uniform Hessian bounds on the smooth approximants are required. Their affine normalizations are controlled only after the limiting law and its covariance have been identified.

Section <a href="#tr:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:section">2</a> constructs the normalization and proves the transport energy estimate. Section <a href="#mat:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:section">3</a> decomposes the entropy deficit and proves the smooth case using two scalar propositions. Section <a href="#sc:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:section">[sc:section]</a> proves those propositions; Appendix <a href="#cert:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:verification">8</a> supplies the quantitative one-variable bounds. Section <a href="#geo:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:section">5</a> removes the smoothness assumptions. Section <a href="#sec:rigidity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rigidity">6</a> classifies equality in the entropy bound. Section <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">7</a> applies that classification to the exponential density on the cone over $K$, completing Theorem <a href="#intro:simplex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:simplex">1</a>.

<a id="tr:section"></a>
### Gaussian transport and a matrix surplus

<a id="tr:smooth-assumptions"></a>
We first work with a probability density $e^{-V}$ on $\mathbb R^m$ satisfying $$\label{tr:smooth-assumptions}
 V\in C^\infty(\mathbb R^m),\qquad
 0<c_- I\le D^2V\le c_+I<\infty.$$ The constants $c_-,c_+$ may depend on the density. Write $C=\operatorname{Cov}(\mu)$, where $d\mu=e^{-V}\,dy$, and let $\gamma_m$ be standard Gaussian measure. Throughout this section, $\mathbb E$ denotes integration against $\gamma_m$.

There are two objectives. We choose affine coordinates in which a reparametrization of the transport Jacobian has mean zero. We then use convexity of $V$ to control derivatives of that reparametrization. The resulting estimate contains an additional nonnegative term measuring separation between eigenvalues; retaining this term is essential in the matrix argument that follows.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The Gaussian parametrization

<a id="tr:scalar-functions"></a>
Let $\Phi$ and $\varphi$ denote the distribution function and density of a standard one-dimensional Gaussian. Define, for $a\in\mathbb R$, $$\label{tr:scalar-functions}
 \begin{gathered}
 t(a)=-\log\Phi(-a),\qquad q(a)=t'(a)=\frac{\varphi(a)}{\Phi(-a)},
 \qquad d(a)=q(a)-a,\\
 k(a)=\frac{t(a)}{q(a)},\qquad b(a)=k'(a),\qquad l(a)=b'(a).
 \end{gathered}$$ The map $t$ transports a standard Gaussian to the exponential law of mean one: $\Phi(-G)$ is uniform on $(0,1)$ when $G$ is standard Gaussian. Thus $q$ is the derivative of the scalar transport associated with the entropy benchmark $h=1$, $\operatorname{Var}=1$.

<a id="tr:scalar-identities"></a>
The identities $$\label{tr:scalar-identities}
 q'=qd,\qquad b=1-kd,\qquad l=k-q+ab$$ follow by differentiation. In particular, $q-k=ab-l$. We record the elementary properties that permit us to use $q$ as a change of variable.

<a id="stmt-tr-q-properties"></a>
<div id="tr:q-properties" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *The function $q$ is a smooth increasing bijection from $\mathbb R$ to $(0,\infty)$. Moreover, $$d>0,\qquad 0<q'<1,\qquad q''\ge0,
 \qquad 0<1-qd\le d^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tr-q-properties"><summary>Proof of stmt-tr-q-properties</summary>

*Proof.* Let $X_a$ have the conditional law of $G-a$ given $G>a$, and write $\mathbb E_a$ for expectation under this law. Gaussian integration by parts gives $$\mathbb E_a X_a=d(a),\qquad
 \operatorname{Var}(X_a)=1-q(a)d(a).$$ Since $X_a>0$ almost surely, $d>0$ and hence $q'=qd>0$. Its strictly positive variance gives $q'<1$.

For completeness, this variance is at most the square of its mean. The survival function $$S_a(u)=\frac{\Phi(-a-u)}{\Phi(-a)},\qquad u\ge0,$$ satisfies $S_a(u+v)\le S_a(u)S_a(v)$, since its hazard rate $q(a+u)$ is increasing. Integrating over $u,v\ge0$ yields $$\frac12\mathbb E_a X_a^2
 =\int_0^\infty uS_a(u)\,du
 \le\left(\int_0^\infty S_a(u)\,du\right)^2=d(a)^2.$$ Consequently $1-qd=\operatorname{Var}(X_a)\le d^2$. Differentiating $q'=qd$ now gives $q''=q(d^2+qd-1)\ge0$. Finally, $q(a)\to0$ as $a\to-\infty$, whereas $q(a)>a$ for $a>0$. These limits prove surjectivity. ◻

</details>

<a id="tr:bins"></a>
We shall also need two positivity bounds and a quantitative growth estimate. Their proof, including the rational bounds on the compact interval and estimates on both tails, is given in Appendix <a href="#cert:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:verification">8</a>. Put <a id="tr:M-definition"></a>$$\label{tr:M-definition}
 D_0(a)=\frac1{d(a)},\qquad M(a)=D_0(a)^2(1-b(a)^2).$$ A lower bound for the growth of $M$ in $\log q$ will let us compare uniform averages in $q$ with uniform averages in $\log q$ in the surplus estimate. Define a nondecreasing step function $m_0$ by the following table. At a finite endpoint we take the value in the interval to its right. $$\label{tr:bins}
\begin{array}{c|rrrrrr}
 &O&P_1&R_1&U_1&V_1&T_1\\\hline
 a&(-\infty,-3)&[-3,-2)&[-2,-1)&[-1,\tfrac12)&[\tfrac12,4)&[4,\infty)\\
 m_0(a)&0&.19&.38&.68&.68&.68
\end{array}$$ All terminating decimals in this paper denote exact rational numbers.

<a id="stmt-tr-scalar-basic"></a>
<div id="tr:scalar-basic" class="lemma">

<a id="tr:scalar-basic-equations"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Scalar bounds). *The functions in <a href="#tr:scalar-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:scalar-functions">[tr:scalar-functions]</a> satisfy, on $\mathbb R$, $$\label{tr:scalar-basic-equations}
 0<b<\frac12,\qquad l>0,\qquad
 \frac{d\log M}{d\log q}
 :=\frac{M'}{Md}\ge m_0.$$*

</div>

For a real symmetric matrix, scalar functions will always be interpreted by spectral functional calculus. If $f$ is differentiable, its divided difference is <a id="tr:divided-difference"></a>$$\label{tr:divided-difference}
 f[u,v]=\begin{cases}(f(u)-f(v))/(u-v),&u\ne v,\\f'(u),&u=v.
 \end{cases}$$ The classical Daleckii–Krein formula (Daleckii and Krein 1965) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DaleckiiKrein1965](#ref-DaleckiiKrein1965)] states that, in an eigenbasis of a symmetric matrix $A$, the derivative of $f(A)$ in a symmetric direction $Z$ has entries $f[a_i,a_j]Z_{ij}$; see also (Noferini 2017, sec. 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Noferini2017](#ref-Noferini2017)]. This formula will allow us to keep the off-diagonal entries of matrix derivatives.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Transport and the choice of coordinates

<a id="tr:Jacobian-bounds"></a>
Brenier’s theorem gives a convex potential $\phi$ such that $(\nabla\phi)_\#\gamma_m=\mu$; its gradient is unique up to null sets (Brenier 1991; McCann 1995) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Brenier1991](#ref-Brenier1991), [McCann1995](#ref-McCann1995)]. For $m\ge2$ we use the full-space regularity theorem of Cordero-Erausquin and Figalli (Cordero-Erausquin and Figalli 2019, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[CorderoErausquinFigalli2019](#ref-CorderoErausquinFigalli2019)], which extends Caffarelli’s interior theory (Caffarelli 1992) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Caffarelli1992](#ref-Caffarelli1992)] to the unbounded domains considered here. It applies with both domains equal to $\mathbb R^m$, because both densities are smooth and locally bounded away from zero. For $m=1$, the same smoothness follows directly by expressing monotone transport through the two distribution functions. Thus $\phi$ is smooth in every dimension. Caffarelli’s contraction theorem (Caffarelli 2000) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Caffarelli2000](#ref-Caffarelli2000)] and its inverse bound then imply that the Jacobian <a id="tr:Jacobian"></a>$$\label{tr:Jacobian}
 J=D^2\phi$$ satisfies $$\label{tr:Jacobian-bounds}
 c_+^{-1/2}I\le J\le c_-^{-1/2}I.$$ For the lower bound, apply the contraction theorem to the inverse transport. We use its quantitative form: a source potential with Hessian at most $aI$ and a target potential with Hessian at least $bI$ give a transport Lipschitz constant at most $\sqrt{a/b}$; see also (Chewi and Pooladian 2023, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[ChewiPooladian2023](#ref-ChewiPooladian2023)].

Define the symmetric matrix field <a id="tr:A-definition"></a>$$\label{tr:A-definition}
 A=q^{-1}(J).$$ The bounds <a href="#tr:Jacobian-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:Jacobian-bounds">[tr:Jacobian-bounds]</a> place the spectrum of $A$ in a compact interval. Our preferred coordinates are characterized by $\mathbb EA=0$. They need not be the usual isotropic coordinates.

<a id="stmt-tr-normalization"></a>
<div id="tr:normalization" class="proposition">

**Proposition 5** (Affine normalization). *Let $\mu=e^{-V}dy$ satisfy <a href="#tr:smooth-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:smooth-assumptions">[tr:smooth-assumptions]</a>. There exists a positive definite symmetric matrix $P$ such that the Brenier map from $\gamma_m$ to $P_\#\mu$ has Jacobian $J_P$ satisfying $$\mathbb E q^{-1}(J_P)=0.$$ The transformed density again satisfies <a href="#tr:smooth-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:smooth-assumptions">[tr:smooth-assumptions]</a>, with possibly different positive constants.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tr-normalization"><summary>Proof of stmt-tr-normalization</summary>

<a id="tr:homotopy-covariance"></a>
*Proof.* We use a homotopy from the Gaussian and show that its zeros stay in a compact subset of the positive definite cone. For $0\le\theta\le1$, define $$W_\theta(y)=(1-\theta)|y|^2/2+\theta V(y),\qquad
 d\mu_\theta=Z_\theta^{-1}e^{-W_\theta(y)}\,dy,$$ where $Z_\theta$ is the normalizing integral. There are constants $a_-,a_+>0$, independent of $\theta$, with $a_-I\le D^2W_\theta\le a_+I$. These densities have uniformly Gaussian tails. Their covariance matrices $C_\theta$ depend continuously on $\theta$, are positive definite, and therefore satisfy $$\label{tr:homotopy-covariance}
 c_0I\le C_\theta\le C_0I$$ for fixed $c_0,C_0>0$.

<a id="tr:homotopy-spectral"></a>
For a positive definite symmetric $P$, let $J_{\theta,P}$ be the Jacobian of the transport to $P_\#\mu_\theta$. Define a map with values in the vector space of symmetric matrices by $$F(\theta,P)=\mathbb E q^{-1}(J_{\theta,P}).$$ Contraction in both directions gives $$\label{tr:homotopy-spectral}
 \frac{\lambda_{\min}(P)}{\sqrt{a_+}}I
 \le J_{\theta,P}\le\frac{\|P\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\sqrt{a_-}}I.$$

We first verify continuity of $F$. Restrict $P$ to any compact subset of the positive definite cone. The transports have common Lipschitz constants by <a href="#tr:homotopy-spectral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:homotopy-spectral">[tr:homotopy-spectral]</a>; their values at zero are bounded because their means and second moments are bounded. Every convergent sequence $(\theta_j,P_j)$ thus has locally uniformly convergent subsequences of transport maps. Their limits push forward $\gamma_m$ to the limiting target and remain gradients of convex functions, so Brenier uniqueness identifies the limit. Write $J_j,J$ for the corresponding Jacobians. They converge weak-\* locally and obey common positive spectral bounds.

The change-of-variables equation expresses $\log\det J_j$ in terms of the target potential composed with its transport map. It therefore converges locally uniformly to $\log\det J$. On a fixed compact spectral interval, strict concavity of $\log\det$ gives a constant $c>0$ such that $$\log\det J_j\le\log\det J+
 \operatorname{tr}\bigl(J^{-1}(J_j-J)\bigr)-c|J_j-J|_{\mathrm F}^2.$$ Integration on any ball, followed by weak-\* convergence, proves $J_j\to J$ in local $L^2$. Functional calculus then gives local $L^2$ convergence of $q^{-1}(J_j)$. Their common spectral bounds control the Gaussian tails, proving continuity of $F$.

We next bound all zeros, uniformly in $\theta$. Suppose $\mathbb EA=0$, where $A=q^{-1}(J_{\theta,P})$. Convexity of $q$ and Jensen’s inequality on the expected spectral measure of each unit vector give $$\mathbb E J_{\theta,P}\ge q(0)I.$$ Gaussian integration by parts gives $\mathbb E(x_r\partial_i\phi)=(\mathbb E J_{\theta,P})_{ir}$. Orthogonal projection of the centered components of $\nabla\phi$ onto the span of $x_1,\ldots,x_m$ consequently yields $$P C_\theta P\ge(\mathbb E J_{\theta,P})^2\ge q(0)^2I.$$ Together with <a href="#tr:homotopy-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:homotopy-covariance">[tr:homotopy-covariance]</a>, this bounds $P^{-1}$ uniformly. The lower estimate in <a href="#tr:homotopy-spectral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:homotopy-spectral">[tr:homotopy-spectral]</a> now gives a uniform lower bound $A\ge a_*I$, where we may take $a_*\le0$. Since $q'\le1$, $$q(a)\le q(a_*)+a-a_*\quad(a\ge a_*),
 \qquad
 \mathbb E\operatorname{tr}J_{\theta,P}
 \le m\bigl(q(a_*)-a_*\bigr).$$ On the other hand, Gaussian Poincaré and <a href="#tr:homotopy-spectral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:homotopy-spectral">[tr:homotopy-spectral]</a> imply $$c_0\|P\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2
 \le\operatorname{tr}(P C_\theta P)
 \le\mathbb E\operatorname{tr}J_{\theta,P}^2
 \le\frac{\|P\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\sqrt{a_-}}
       \mathbb E\operatorname{tr}J_{\theta,P}.$$ This also bounds $P$ uniformly.

Choose a bounded open set in the space of symmetric matrices whose closure lies in the positive definite cone and whose interior contains every zero just bounded. The map $F(\theta,\cdot)$ has no zeros on its boundary. At $\theta=0$, the target is $N(0,P^2)$, so $J_{0,P}=P$ and $F(0,P)=q^{-1}(P)$. Its unique zero is $q(0)I$; its derivative there is multiplication by the positive scalar $1/q'(0)$. Its Brouwer degree is therefore nonzero. Homotopy invariance of degree gives a zero at $\theta=1$, as required. ◻

</details>

The quantity $m+\tfrac12\log\det\operatorname{Cov}(\mu)-h(\mu)$ is unchanged by an invertible affine transformation. Indeed, a linear map $P$ adds $\log|\det P|$ to entropy and $2\log|\det P|$ to the covariance log determinant. We may therefore impose the normalization from Proposition <a href="#tr:normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:normalization">5</a>. From now on $V,J,A,C$ refer to this transformed density and transport, and <a id="tr:mean-zero"></a>$$\label{tr:mean-zero}
 \mathbb EA=0.$$

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The transport equation and its energy

We shall differentiate the Jacobian and integrate the resulting equation. The twice-differentiated change-of-variables equation is part of Caffarelli’s transport method (Caffarelli 2000, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Caffarelli2000](#ref-Caffarelli2000)]. The next lemma establishes the finite energy needed to do this without additional decay assumptions on higher derivatives of $V$. For a matrix field $F$, set $$|F|_{\mathrm F}^2=\operatorname{tr}(F^{\mathsf T}F),\qquad
 \|F\|^2=\mathbb E|F|_{\mathrm F}^2,\qquad
 \|\partial F\|^2=\sum_{r=1}^m\|\partial_rF\|^2.$$ The same convention sums over any displayed derivative index of a collection of matrix fields.

<a id="stmt-tr-energy"></a>
<div id="tr:energy" class="lemma">

<a id="tr:Jacobian-equation"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Jacobian equation and finite energy). *Under <a href="#tr:smooth-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:smooth-assumptions">[tr:smooth-assumptions]</a>, put $J_r=\partial_rJ$ and $$\mathcal L u=\operatorname{tr}(J^{-1}D^2u)
             -\nabla V(\nabla\phi)\cdot\nabla u.$$ Then $$\label{tr:Jacobian-equation}
 \mathcal L J_{ij}
 =-\delta_{ij}+(JD^2V(\nabla\phi)J)_{ij}
   +\operatorname{tr}(J^{-1}J_iJ^{-1}J_j).$$ Moreover $\|\partial J\|<\infty$. The matrix fields $A$ and $k(A)$ belong to Gaussian $H^1$, and the integrations by parts below with smooth matrix functions of $J$ are justified by compact cutoffs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tr-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-tr-energy</summary>

<a id="tr:density-equation"></a>
<a id="tr:divergence-form"></a>
*Proof.* The density equation is $$\label{tr:density-equation}
 \log\det J=V(\nabla\phi)-|x|^2/2-(m/2)\log(2\pi).$$ Differentiating twice gives <a href="#tr:Jacobian-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:Jacobian-equation">[tr:Jacobian-equation]</a>. The divergence of the cofactor matrix of a Hessian vanishes. Applying this identity to $J$, and then differentiating <a href="#tr:density-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:density-equation">[tr:density-equation]</a> once, shows that $$\mathcal L u=e^{|x|^2/2}\operatorname{div}
       \bigl(e^{-|x|^2/2}J^{-1}\nabla u\bigr).$$ Thus, for smooth $u$ and compactly supported smooth $v$, $$\label{tr:divergence-form}
 \mathbb E(v\mathcal Lu)
 =-\mathbb E\langle\nabla v,J^{-1}\nabla u\rangle.$$

Choose $0<\lambda\le\Lambda<\infty$ such that $\lambda I\le J\le\Lambda I$. Taking the trace in <a href="#tr:Jacobian-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:Jacobian-equation">[tr:Jacobian-equation]</a>, and using $D^2V\ge0$, gives $$\mathcal L(\operatorname{tr}J)
 \ge-m+\Lambda^{-2}\sum_r|J_r|_{\mathrm F}^2.$$ For a compactly supported cutoff $0\le\chi\le1$, integration by parts and $|\nabla\operatorname{tr}J|\le\sqrt m\,|\partial J|$ imply $$\begin{aligned}
 \Lambda^{-2}\mathbb E\chi^2|\partial J|^2
 &\le m+2\lambda^{-1}\sqrt m\,
             \mathbb E\bigl(\chi|\nabla\chi|\,|\partial J|\bigr)\\
 &\le m+\tfrac12\Lambda^{-2}\mathbb E\chi^2|\partial J|^2
       +2m\Lambda^2\lambda^{-2}\mathbb E|\nabla\chi|^2.
\end{aligned}$$ Let $\chi$ tend to one through standard radial cutoffs with gradients bounded by a constant divided by their radii. Fatou’s lemma proves $\|\partial J\|<\infty$.

All eigenvalues of $J$ lie in $[\lambda,\Lambda]$. The Fréchet derivatives of $q^{-1}$ and of $k\circ q^{-1}$ are bounded on that spectral interval, so $A,k(A)\in H^1(\gamma_m)$. The same applies to any smooth scalar function on this interval. For such a matrix multiplier, insert a cutoff into <a href="#tr:divergence-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:divergence-form">[tr:divergence-form]</a> and let it tend to one. The terms containing its gradient vanish by Cauchy–Schwarz and the energy bound; the remaining differentiated terms are bounded by a constant times $1+|\partial J|^2$. This justifies the asserted integrations by parts. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Keeping the eigenvalue separation

Set $Z_r=\partial_r A$. At each point diagonalize $A$, and write $a_i$ for its eigenvalues and $\lambda_i=q(a_i)$ for those of $J$. For real $a,b$, define <a id="tr:surplus-weight"></a>$$\label{tr:surplus-weight}
 \begin{gathered}
 \delta(a,b)=|\log q(a)-\log q(b)|,\qquad
 m_*(a,b)=m_0(\min(a,b)),\\
 r(a,b)=\frac{1+m_*(a,b)}{16}
    \left(1+\frac{\delta(a,b)^2}{30}
             +\frac{\delta(a,b)^4}{1680}\right).
 \end{gathered}$$ We use the abbreviations $\delta_{ij}=\delta(a_i,a_j)$ and $r_{ij}=r(a_i,a_j)$. The quantity <a id="tr:surplus-definition"></a>$$\label{tr:surplus-definition}
 \mathcal C=\mathbb E\sum_{i,j,r}
           r_{ij}\delta_{ij}^2(Z_r)_{ij}^2$$ is independent of the choice of orthonormal basis within repeated eigenspaces. In particular, the factor $\delta_{ij}^2$ vanishes when $a_i=a_j$.

<a id="stmt-tr-surplus"></a>
<div id="tr:surplus" class="proposition">

<a id="tr:surplus-inequality"></a>
**Proposition 7** (Matrix surplus). *For the transport satisfying <a href="#tr:smooth-assumptions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:smooth-assumptions">[tr:smooth-assumptions]</a>, with the notation above, $$\label{tr:surplus-inequality}
 \mathbb E\operatorname{tr}k(A)^2
 \ge\|\partial A\|^2-\|\partial k(A)\|^2+\mathcal C.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-tr-surplus"><summary>Proof of stmt-tr-surplus</summary>

*Proof.* The proof has two parts. Symmetry of the third derivatives of $\phi$ first replaces the transport equation by averages of the scalar function $M$. Comparing arithmetic and logarithmic averages then produces the term $\mathcal C$.

<a id="tr:initial-multiplier"></a>
The identity $qk=t$ will match the entropy term in the covariance completion of Section <a href="#mat:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:section">3</a>; we first estimate the derivative cost of $k(A)$. Put $F(\lambda)=k(q^{-1}(\lambda))^2$. Multiply <a href="#tr:Jacobian-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:Jacobian-equation">[tr:Jacobian-equation]</a> by $F(J)$ in trace and integrate by parts. Lemma <a href="#tr:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:energy">6</a> justifies this operation. At a fixed point choose an orthonormal eigenbasis of $J$ and rotate all three indices of the symmetric tensor $\Theta_{ijr}=\partial_rJ_{ij}=\partial_i\partial_j\partial_r\phi$ into that basis. The quadratic term on the right of the Jacobian equation has trace contraction $$\sum_i F(\lambda_i)\operatorname{tr}(J^{-1}J_iJ^{-1}J_i)
 =\sum_{i,j,r}\frac{F(\lambda_i)}{\lambda_j\lambda_r}\Theta_{ijr}^2,$$ where we used the symmetry of $\Theta$. The term obtained by integration by parts is $$\sum_r\lambda_r^{-1}\operatorname{tr}\bigl((\partial_rF(J))J_r\bigr)
 =\sum_{i,j,r}\frac{F[\lambda_i,\lambda_j]}{\lambda_r}\Theta_{ijr}^2.$$ The remaining term is $\operatorname{tr}(F(J)JD^2VJ)\ge0$. Discarding it gives $$\label{tr:initial-multiplier}
 \mathbb E\operatorname{tr}k(A)^2
 \ge\mathbb E\sum_{i,j,r}\Theta_{ijr}^2
 \left(\frac{F(\lambda_i)}{\lambda_j\lambda_r}
             +\frac{F[\lambda_i,\lambda_j]}{\lambda_r}\right).$$ Only pointwise changes of basis are made here; no eigenvectors are differentiated.

<a id="tr:arithmetic-energy"></a>
Regard $M$ as a function of $\lambda=q(a)$. The identities <a href="#tr:scalar-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:scalar-identities">[tr:scalar-identities]</a> give <a id="tr:lambdaF"></a>$$\label{tr:lambdaF}
 \frac{d}{d\lambda}(\lambda F(\lambda))
 =k^2+\frac{2kb}{d}
 =\frac{1-b^2}{d^2}=M.$$ For two positive numbers $u,v$, let $M^{\mathrm{ar}}_{uv}$ be the uniform average of $M$ on the interval between $u$ and $v$, with its continuous interpretation when $u=v$. With the weight $\Theta_{ijr}^2/(\lambda_i\lambda_j\lambda_r)$, symmetrization in $i,j$ changes the coefficient in <a href="#tr:initial-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:initial-multiplier">[tr:initial-multiplier]</a> to $$\frac{\lambda_i+\lambda_j}{2}\,
 M^{\mathrm{ar}}_{\lambda_i\lambda_j}.$$ In the full sum we can replace this coefficient by $\lambda_rM^{\mathrm{ar}}_{\lambda_i\lambda_j}$, decreasing its value. To see this, order three eigenvalues as $u\le v\le w$. Lemma <a href="#tr:scalar-basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:scalar-basic">4</a> makes $M$ increasing, so $$M^{\mathrm{ar}}_{uv}\le M^{\mathrm{ar}}_{uw}
                         \le M^{\mathrm{ar}}_{vw}.$$ The ordered coefficients $u+v-2w$, $u+w-2v$, and $v+w-2u$ sum to zero. Their scalar product with these three ordered averages is nonnegative, by the rearrangement inequality. Full symmetry of $\Theta$ permits precisely this averaging over permutations of the three indices. We have proved $$\label{tr:arithmetic-energy}
 \mathbb E\operatorname{tr}k(A)^2
 \ge\mathbb E\sum_{i,j,r}
 \frac{\Theta_{ijr}^2}{\lambda_i\lambda_j\lambda_r}\,
 \lambda_r M^{\mathrm{ar}}_{\lambda_i\lambda_j}.$$

<a id="tr:derivative-coefficients"></a>
<a id="tr:logarithmic-chebyshev"></a>
We now fix a pair of eigenvalues. Bars in the rest of the proof denote uniform averages in $\log\lambda$ over their interval. Set $$x=\delta_{ij}/2,\qquad w_0=\frac{x}{\sinh x},\qquad m_*=m_*(a_i,a_j),$$ with $w_0=1$ at $x=0$. Since $da/d\log\lambda=D_0$, the divided-difference formula shows that the coefficients of $(Z_r)_{ij}^2$ and $(\partial_r k(A))_{ij}^2$, respectively, when written with the same weight as in <a href="#tr:arithmetic-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:arithmetic-energy">[tr:arithmetic-energy]</a>, are $$\label{tr:derivative-coefficients}
 \lambda_r w_0^2\overline{D_0}^{\,2},\qquad
 \lambda_r w_0^2\overline{bD_0}^{\,2}.$$ Both $D_0(1+b)$ and $D_0(1-b)=k$ are increasing: $d'=qd-1<0$, and $b'=l>0$, $k'=b>0$. The covariance inequality for two increasing functions on an interval therefore yields $$\label{tr:logarithmic-chebyshev}
 \overline M\ge
 M_1:=\overline{D_0}^{\,2}-\overline{bD_0}^{\,2}
 \ge\tfrac34\overline{D_0}^{\,2}.$$

We also need a quantitative comparison of the two averaging measures. On the logarithmic interval, <a href="#tr:scalar-basic-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:scalar-basic-equations">[tr:scalar-basic-equations]</a> says that $M(\lambda)\lambda^{-m_*}$ is increasing. Exponential weighting and the same covariance inequality give <a id="tr:average-comparison"></a>$$\label{tr:average-comparison}
 \frac{M^{\mathrm{ar}}_{\lambda_i\lambda_j}}{\overline M}
 \ge\frac{\operatorname{shc}((1+m_*)x)}
          {\operatorname{shc}(x)\operatorname{shc}(m_*x)},
 \qquad \operatorname{shc}(x)=\frac{\sinh x}{x}.$$ Indeed, after centering the logarithmic interval, the left side is $\mathbb E_0(e^sM)/[\mathbb E_0e^s\,\mathbb E_0M]$, where $\mathbb E_0$ is its uniform average. Under the measure proportional to $e^{m_*s}$, the increasing factor $Me^{-m_*s}$ can only increase the mean of $e^s$. Taking $M=e^{m_*s}$ gives the displayed ratio.

<a id="tr:hyperbolic-comparison"></a>
Here is a convenient explicit lower bound for this ratio. Define $A_0(x)=x\coth x-1$, with $A_0(0)=0$. The identity $$x\coth x+\frac{x^2}{\sinh^2x}\ge2$$ implies both $A_0(x)\ge1-w_0^2$ and that $A_0(x)/x^2$ is nonincreasing for $x>0$. To verify the identity, clear the denominator and expand $\tfrac{x}{2}\sinh(2x)+x^2-\cosh(2x)+1$ in powers of $x$; the coefficients vanish through degree four and are nonnegative thereafter. Since $0\le m_*\le1$, we obtain $A_0(m_*x)\ge m_*^2A_0(x)$. The addition formula for $\sinh$ now gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{\operatorname{shc}((1+m_*)x)}
          {\operatorname{shc}(x)\operatorname{shc}(m_*x)}
 &=1+\frac{m_*A_0(x)+A_0(m_*x)}{1+m_*}\notag\\
 &\ge1+m_*(1-w_0^2).
 \label{tr:hyperbolic-comparison}
\end{aligned}$$ Every formula here extends continuously to $m_*=0$ or $x=0$.

Combining <a href="#tr:logarithmic-chebyshev" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:logarithmic-chebyshev">[tr:logarithmic-chebyshev]</a>–<a href="#tr:hyperbolic-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:hyperbolic-comparison">[tr:hyperbolic-comparison]</a>, the amount by which the coefficient in <a href="#tr:arithmetic-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:arithmetic-energy">[tr:arithmetic-energy]</a> exceeds the difference of the two coefficients in <a href="#tr:derivative-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:derivative-coefficients">[tr:derivative-coefficients]</a> is at least $$\lambda_r(1+m_*)(1-w_0^2)M_1.$$ Relative to the coefficient of $(Z_r)_{ij}^2$, this is at least $$\frac34(1+m_*)(w_0^{-2}-1)
 \ge\frac{1+m_*}{16}
    \left(\delta_{ij}^2+\frac{\delta_{ij}^4}{30}
                            +\frac{\delta_{ij}^6}{1680}\right).$$ The last inequality is the Taylor expansion of $(\sinh x/x)^2$, whose omitted coefficients are positive. Substituting in <a href="#tr:arithmetic-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:arithmetic-energy">[tr:arithmetic-energy]</a> and summing proves <a href="#tr:surplus-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:surplus-inequality">[tr:surplus-inequality]</a>. ◻

</details>

We have obtained two inputs for the entropy argument: the matrix field $A$ has no constant Gaussian component, and convexity supplies <a href="#tr:surplus-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:surplus-inequality">[tr:surplus-inequality]</a>. The next section combines these facts with a Gaussian covariance identity and separates the first chaos of $A$ from its higher chaoses.

<a id="mat:section"></a>
### Gaussian decomposition of the entropy deficit

The transport estimate of Proposition <a href="#tr:surplus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:surplus">7</a> controls the Gaussian energy of the reparametrized Jacobian. Its degree-one part, however, has no Poincaré surplus. We use that part to choose a matrix supporting plane for the log determinant. The remaining Gaussian chaoses then supply the coercivity needed to prove the entropy bound. We retain a strict remainder that will also identify the equality cases. Throughout this section, $\mathbb E$ denotes integration against the standard Gaussian measure $\gamma_m$.

<a id="mat:rigidity-weight"></a>
Define a scalar weight on $[0,\infty)$ by $$\label{mat:rigidity-weight}
 \omega(\lambda)=
 \frac{3(\lambda-1)^2}{37+3\lambda}
 \left(\frac3{20}+\frac{49}{100}\frac{37}{37+3\lambda}\right)
 \min\left\{\frac{13}{100},\frac{32}{25\sqrt\lambda}\right\},$$ where the minimum is $13/100$ at $\lambda=0$. This weight is continuous and nonnegative, vanishes only at $1$, and satisfies $\omega(\lambda)/\sqrt\lambda\to24/125$ as $\lambda\to\infty$.

<a id="stmt-prop-slack"></a>
<div id="prop:slack" class="theorem">

<a id="mat:strict-remainder"></a>
**Theorem 8** (The smooth entropy bound with a remainder). *<span id="mat:entropy-smooth" label="mat:entropy-smooth"></span>Let $m\ge1$ be an integer and let $\mu=e^{-V}\,\,\mathrm dy$ be a probability measure on $\mathbb R^m$, where $V\in C^\infty(\mathbb R^m)$ and $c_-I\le D^2V\le c_+I$ for constants $0<c_-\le c_+<\infty$. Choose the affine normalization of Proposition <a href="#tr:normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:normalization">5</a>, and let $A=q^{-1}(J)$ be the resulting Gaussian transport matrix field, so $\mathbb EA=0$. Set $$X_r=\mathbb E(x_rA),\qquad Y=A-\sum_{r=1}^m x_rX_r,\qquad
 B=\sum_{r=1}^mX_r^2.$$ If $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_m$ are the eigenvalues of $B$, then $$\label{mat:strict-remainder}
 2h(\mu)-2m-\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(\mu)
 \ge \frac1{500}\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(Y^2)
       +\frac1{1000}\sum_{i=1}^m\omega(\lambda_i).$$ In particular, $$h(\mu)\ge m+\frac12\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(\mu).$$*

</div>

We use the affine normalization of Proposition <a href="#tr:normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:normalization">5</a>: the Brenier Jacobian $J$ and the symmetric matrix field $A=q^{-1}(J)$ satisfy $\mathbb EA=0$. This normalization does not change $h(\mu)-\frac12\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(\mu)$. For the proof, we replace $\mu$ by this affine image and retain the notation $\mu$. Scalar functions of $A$ are interpreted by spectral calculus; when their arguments are omitted, $q,k,t,b,l$ and the functions introduced below are evaluated at $A$. For a matrix field $F$, put $$\tau(F)=\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}F,\qquad
 \|F\|^2=\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(F^tF),\qquad
 \|F\|_S^2=\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(F^tSF)$$ when $S$ is a constant positive definite matrix. For a family indexed by $r$, these squared norms include summation over $r$. Lemma <a href="#tr:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:energy">6</a> provides the Sobolev regularity used below.

<a id="mat:deficit-exact"></a>
Write $C=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(\mu)$ and let $$\mathcal{D}=2m+\log\det C-2h(\mu)$$ be the negative of twice the entropy gap. The transport change of variables and $\log q(a)=-a^2/2-\frac12\log(2\pi)+t(a)$ give $$\label{mat:deficit-exact}
 \mathcal{D}=\tau(A^2-2t)+\log\det C+m.$$

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Covariance and the first Gaussian chaos

Let $N=-\Delta+x\cdot\nabla$ be the nonnegative Ornstein–Uhlenbeck operator and let $R=(I+N)^{-1}$. The degree-$d$ Gaussian chaos is the span of coordinatewise products of one-dimensional Hermite polynomials whose degrees sum to $d$. These spaces give an orthogonal decomposition of $L^2(\gamma_m)$; on the degree-$d$ space, $N$ and $R$ act by $d$ and $(1+d)^{-1}$, respectively. We write $\partial=(\partial_1,\ldots,\partial_m)$ and $\partial^*v=\sum_r(x_rv_r-\partial_rv_r)$, so $N=\partial^*\partial$. The usual Gaussian Sobolev norm is $\|F\|_{H^1}^2=\|F\|^2+\|\partial F\|^2$; its dual norm is $$\|F\|_{-1}^2=\sum_{d\ge0}\frac{\|F_d\|^2}{1+d},$$ initially for square-integrable fields, and then by completion. Here $F_d$ denotes the degree-$d$ chaos component. These spectral conventions are standard; see (Nourdin and Peccati 2009, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[NourdinPeccati2009](#ref-NourdinPeccati2009)].

We use the Gaussian Helffer–Sjöstrand covariance representation; see (Duerinckx and Otto 2020, Proposition 4.1(ii)) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[DuerinckxOtto2020](#ref-DuerinckxOtto2020)]. We include a direct chaos proof below. The term $-2\tau(t)$ in <a href="#mat:deficit-exact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:deficit-exact">[mat:deficit-exact]</a> determines its completion. Since $qk=t$, subtracting $(I+N)k$ from $q$ in the covariance quadratic form produces exactly the cross term $-2t$.

<a id="stmt-mat-covariance"></a>
<div id="mat:covariance" class="lemma">

<a id="mat:covariance-completion"></a>
<a id="mat:dual-bound"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Covariance completion). *Define the symmetric $H^{-1}$ matrix field $$U=q(A)-(I+N)k(A)$$ and let $W$ be the Gram matrix of its rows in $H^{-1}$: $$W_{ij}=\sum_{\ell=1}^m
       \langle U_{i\ell},U_{j\ell}\rangle_{-1}.$$ Then $$\label{mat:covariance-completion}
 C=\mathbb E\bigl[J(RJ)\bigr]
   =W+\mathbb E\left(2t-k^2-\sum_r K_r^2\right),
 \qquad K_r=\partial_r k(A).$$ Moreover, if $U=u-\partial^*v$ with $u$ and the family $v=(v_r)_r$ square-integrable, then for every constant $S>0$, $$\label{mat:dual-bound}
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(SW)\le\|u\|_S^2+\|v\|_S^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mat-covariance"><summary>Proof of stmt-mat-covariance</summary>

*Proof.* Put $F=\nabla\phi$. For two degree-$d$ components of $F$, Gaussian integration by parts gives $$\sum_\ell\mathbb E[(\partial_\ell F_{i,d})(\partial_\ell F_{j,d})]
 =d\,\mathbb E[F_{i,d}F_{j,d}].$$ Differentiation lowers the degree by one, and $R$ therefore cancels the factor $d$. Summing over $d\ge1$ proves $C_{ij}=\sum_\ell\mathbb E[J_{i\ell}R J_{j\ell}]$. Symmetry of $J$ gives the first matrix identity in <a href="#mat:covariance-completion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:covariance-completion">[mat:covariance-completion]</a>. Expanding the row Gram matrix of $q-(I+N)k$ gives cross terms $-2\mathbb E(qk)=-2\mathbb Et$. The remaining term is $$\mathbb E\bigl[k(I+N)k\bigr]
   =\mathbb E\left(k^2+\sum_r K_r^2\right),$$ by entrywise integration by parts. This proves the second identity. The calculation is valid by the $H^1$–$H^{-1}$ pairing, even if $Nk$ is not square-integrable.

For a test field $F$, the pairing with $u-\partial^*v$ is $\langle u,F\rangle-\langle v,\partial F\rangle$. Cauchy–Schwarz bounds it by $(\|u\|^2+\|v\|^2)^{1/2}\|F\|_{H^1}$. Taking the dual norm proves <a href="#mat:dual-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:dual-bound">[mat:dual-bound]</a> for $S=I$, and applying this argument to $S^{1/2}U$ proves the general case. ◻

</details>

<a id="mat:chaos"></a>
Separate the degree-one part of $A$ by writing $$\label{mat:chaos}
 A=\sum_{r=1}^m x_rX_r+Y,\qquad
 X_r=\mathbb E(x_rA),\qquad B=\sum_rX_r^2.$$ The matrices $X_r$ are constant and symmetric; $Y$ contains only chaoses of degree at least two. We shall use <a id="mat:gradient-fields"></a>$$\label{mat:gradient-fields}
 Z_r=\partial_rA=X_r+G_r,\qquad
 G_r=\partial_rY,\qquad
 g_r=\partial_rN^{-1}Y,
 \qquad V_0=\|G\|^2-\|Y\|^2.$$ Thus $\partial^*g=Y$. All three derivative fields are symmetric.

<a id="mat:dual-constants"></a>
