<a id="mat:dual-matrices"></a>
<a id="mat:dual-relations"></a>
For comparison, the coordinatewise Gaussian-to-exponential transport satisfies $$A(x)=\operatorname{diag}(x_1,\ldots,x_m),\qquad B=I.$$ We therefore choose the supporting plane for $\log\det C$ as a regularized inverse of $B$ centered at this value. Fix $$\label{mat:dual-constants}
 \alpha=\frac{3}{40},\qquad s=\frac1{1-\alpha},$$ and set $$\label{mat:dual-matrices}
 S=(I/s+\alpha B)^{-1},\qquad T=S-I,
 \qquad H=-T(B-I).$$ These constant matrices commute with one another, though generally not with functions of $A$. Directly from their definition, $$\label{mat:dual-relations}
 0<S\le sI,\qquad T=-\alpha S(B-I),\qquad
 H=\alpha S(B-I)^2\ge0,\qquad T^2=\alpha HS.$$ In particular, $S=I$ when $B=I$. Deviations of the first chaos from this value produce the nonnegative matrix $H$.

<a id="mat:deficit-reduction"></a>
Concavity of the log determinant yields $\log\det C+m\le\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(SC)-\log\det S$. Combining this with Lemma <a href="#mat:covariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:covariance">9</a> and Proposition <a href="#tr:surplus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:surplus">7</a>, and using $\|A\|^2-\|Z\|^2=-V_0$, gives $$\label{mat:deficit-reduction}
 \mathcal{D}\le -V_0-\mathcal C+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(T-\log S)+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(SW)
   +\tau\left[T\left(2t-k^2-I-\sum_rK_r^2\right)\right].$$ Here $\mathcal C$ is the eigenvalue-separation surplus in Proposition <a href="#tr:surplus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:surplus">7</a>. The remaining task is to estimate the last two terms while retaining the negative contribution involving $H$.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### An exact expansion with noncommuting factors

<a id="mat:residuals"></a>
Recall the divided differences from Section <a href="#tr:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:section">2</a>, with $v[a,a]=v'(a)$. For a smooth scalar function $v$, define its secant increment at the second endpoint by $$\Delta v_{ij}=v[a_i,a_j]-v'(a_j).$$ We use the matrix derivative formula in (Noferini 2017, sec. 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Noferini2017](#ref-Noferini2017)]. At a fixed point, choose an orthonormal eigenbasis of $A$. Entrywise multiplication is denoted by $\circ$. Define the derivative residuals $$\label{mat:residuals}
 D_r=K_r-X_rb=G_rb+Z_r\circ\Delta k,\qquad
 L_r=\partial_rb-X_rl=G_rl+Z_r\circ\Delta b.$$ These matrices need not be symmetric. The distinction between $D_r$ and $D_r^t$ will be essential below.

<a id="mat:constants"></a>
<a id="mat:W-bound"></a>
The scalar identity $q-k=ab-l$ implies <a id="mat:U-representation"></a>$$\label{mat:U-representation}
 U=Yb+\sum_rX_rL_r+(B-I)l-\partial^*D.$$ Indeed, $\partial^*(Xb)=(A-Y)b-Bl-\sum_rX_rL_r$. To divide the higher-chaos terms between the two squares, fix $$\label{mat:constants}
 \beta=\frac7{25},\qquad c=\frac32,\qquad \gamma=\frac c2.$$ Using $\partial^*g=Y$ in <a href="#mat:dual-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:dual-bound">[mat:dual-bound]</a>, we obtain $$\label{mat:W-bound}
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(SW)\le
 \left\|Y(b-\beta)+\sum_rX_rL_r+(B-I)l\right\|_S^2
 +\|D-\beta g\|_S^2.$$

<a id="mat:scalar-functions"></a>
We group the expansion according to its $H$ term and its derivative residuals. In the remaining $Y$ coefficient, we split off $ck$: the identity $\partial^*g=Y$ will transfer $c\tau(TYk)$ to derivatives, where completing the square produces $D-\gamma g$. Introduce the scalar functions $$\label{mat:scalar-functions}
 \begin{aligned}
 f&=k(1+b),&
 h_0&=f-ck-\frac{2(b-\beta)l}{\alpha},\\
 p&=f'-b^2-\frac{l^2}{\alpha},&
 e&=f'-2b^2-\frac{2l^2}{\alpha}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The identity $$af-f'=2t-k^2-b^2-1$$ follows from $q-k=ab-l$ and $b=1-k(q-a)$. For each pair of eigenvalues of $A$, put $$\psi_{ij}=\Delta f_{ij}-2b_j\Delta k_{ij}
                         -\frac{2l_j}{\alpha}\Delta b_{ij},
 \qquad b_j=b(a_j),\quad l_j=l(a_j),$$ and define <a id="mat:M-w"></a>$$\label{mat:M-w}
 M_r=G_re+Z_r\circ\psi+cg_rb,\qquad w_r=D_r-\gamma g_r.$$ Finally, write <a id="mat:P-definition"></a>$$\label{mat:P-definition}
 P_T=\tau(TbBb-TBb^2).$$

<a id="stmt-mat-expansion"></a>
<div id="mat:expansion" class="lemma">

<a id="mat:expanded-bound"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Matrix expansion). *The sum of the last two terms in <a href="#mat:deficit-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:deficit-reduction">[mat:deficit-reduction]</a> is at most $$\begin{aligned}
 &-\tau(Hp)+\tau\left[T\left(Yh_0+\sum_rX_rM_r\right)\right]
       \label{mat:expanded-bound}\\
 &\quad+\left\|Y(b-\beta)+\sum_rX_rL_r\right\|_S^2
       +\|D-\beta g\|_S^2+\gamma^2\tau\left(T\sum_rg_r^2\right)\nonumber\\
 &\quad-P_T-2\tau\left([T,b]\sum_rX_rw_r\right)
                 -\tau\left(T\sum_rw_r^tw_r\right).\nonumber
\end{aligned}$$ Except for the use of <a href="#mat:W-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:W-bound">[mat:W-bound]</a>, this expansion is exact.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mat-expansion"><summary>Proof of stmt-mat-expansion</summary>

*Proof.* Let $F_0=Y(b-\beta)+\sum_rX_rL_r$. The relations <a href="#mat:dual-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:dual-relations">[mat:dual-relations]</a> give $$\|F_0+(B-I)l\|_S^2
 =\|F_0\|_S^2+\frac1\alpha\tau(Hl^2)
                    -\frac2\alpha\tau(TF_0l).$$ For the cross term, the order of the factors follows from $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(F_0^tTl)=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(TF_0l)$, by transposition and cyclicity. Integration by parts on the linear part of $A$ yields $$\tau(TAf)=\tau\left[T\left(Yf+\sum_rX_r\partial_rf\right)\right],
 \qquad
 \partial_rf=X_rf'+G_rf'+Z_r\circ\Delta f.$$ Together with $af-f'=2t-k^2-b^2-1$, this accounts for the terms involving $f'$ and $f$.

To expand the energy term, use both versions of $K_r=X_rb+D_r=bX_r+D_r^t$: <a id="mat:K-square"></a>$$\label{mat:K-square}
 \tau\left(T\sum_rK_r^2\right)
 =\tau(TbBb)+2\tau\left(Tb\sum_rX_rD_r\right)
             +\tau\left(T\sum_rD_r^tD_r\right).$$ The two cross terms agree under transposition inside the trace. Moving $b$ past $T$, rather than past $X_r$, gives $$\tau(TbX_rD_r)=\tau(TX_rD_rb)+\tau([T,b]X_rD_r).$$ The terms containing $B-I$ now combine as $$\tau\bigl(T(B-I)f'\bigr)+\tau(Tb^2-TbBb)
                 +\frac1\alpha\tau(Hl^2)
 =-\tau(Hp)-P_T.$$ In the other terms, substituting <a href="#mat:residuals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:residuals">[mat:residuals]</a> produces $G_re+Z_r\circ\psi$ and the $Y$ multiplier $f-2(b-\beta)l/\alpha=h_0+ck$.

It remains to handle $c\tau(TYk)$. Since $\partial^*g=Y$, $$\tau(TYk)=\sum_r\tau(TK_rg_r).$$ Here integration by parts first gives $\tau(Tg_rK_r)$; transposition makes the two expressions equal. Substituting $K_r=bX_r+D_r^t$ and moving $b$ past $T$ introduces $cg_rb$ in $M_r$ and replaces $D_r$ by $D_r-\gamma g_r$ in the commutator term. The remaining square is completed by $$-\tau(TD_r^tD_r)+c\tau(TD_r^tg_r)
 =-\tau(Tw_r^tw_r)+\gamma^2\tau(Tg_r^2).$$ This proves <a href="#mat:expanded-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:expanded-bound">[mat:expanded-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The commutator and the weighted squares

The commutator in Lemma <a href="#mat:expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:expansion">10</a> has a compensating quadratic term $P_T$. Keeping that term is what permits a dimension-free estimate without assuming that the Jacobian and the first-chaos matrix commute.

<a id="stmt-mat-commutator"></a>
<div id="mat:commutator" class="lemma">

<a id="mat:commutator-bound"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Commutator estimate). *For the matrices $X_r,B,S,T$ in <a href="#mat:chaos" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:chaos">[mat:chaos]</a> and <a href="#mat:dual-matrices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:dual-matrices">[mat:dual-matrices]</a>, any square-integrable symmetric matrix field $b$, and arbitrary square-integrable matrix fields $w_r$, $$\label{mat:commutator-bound}
 -P_T-2\tau\left([T,b]\sum_rX_rw_r\right)
       -\tau\left(T\sum_rw_r^tw_r\right)
 \le\left(1+\frac s8\right)\|w\|^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-mat-commutator"><summary>Proof of stmt-mat-commutator</summary>

<a id="mat:P-square"></a>
*Proof.* Work pointwise and diagonalize $S$. The relation $B=\alpha^{-1}(S^{-1}-I/s)$ gives $$\label{mat:P-square}
 P_T=\frac1{2\alpha}
       \bigl\|S^{-1/2}[T,b]S^{-1/2}\bigr\|^2.$$ Indeed, the contribution of $b_{ij}^2$ for $i<j$ on either side is $(S_i-S_j)^2/(\alpha S_iS_j)$. The norm in <a href="#mat:P-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:P-square">[mat:P-square]</a> includes expectation. Set $F=\sum_rX_rw_r$ and let $\operatorname{skew}M=(M-M^t)/2$. Completing the square in the skew-symmetric matrix $S^{-1/2}[T,b]S^{-1/2}$ bounds the first two terms on the left of <a href="#mat:commutator-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:commutator-bound">[mat:commutator-bound]</a> by $$2\alpha\bigl\|\operatorname{skew}(S^{1/2}FS^{1/2})\bigr\|^2.$$

For completeness, we estimate this square together with the last term. Write $S_i=su_i$, where $0<u_i\le1$, so $\alpha S_iB_i=1-u_i$. The rows of the block matrix $X=(X_1,\ldots,X_m)$ are orthogonal, since $XX^t=B$ is diagonal. For column $j$ of the vertically stacked matrix $(w_r)_r$, let $z_{ij}$ be its component along the normalized $i$th row of $X$. Zero rows can be completed to orthonormal directions, and cause no contribution to $F$. Then $F_{ij}=\sqrt{B_i}\,z_{ij}$ and the remaining column components are orthogonal to these directions.

For $i<j$, the quadratic form in $(z_{ij},z_{ji})$ has matrix $$\begin{pmatrix}
 1-su_iu_j&-s\sqrt{u_iu_j(1-u_i)(1-u_j)}\\
 -s\sqrt{u_iu_j(1-u_i)(1-u_j)}&1-su_iu_j
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ Its largest eigenvalue is at most $1+s(z-2z^2)\le1+s/8$, where $z=\sqrt{u_iu_j}$; here $(1-u_i)(1-u_j)\le(1-z)^2$. Diagonal and orthogonal column components have coefficient at most $1$. Summation and expectation prove <a href="#mat:commutator-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:commutator-bound">[mat:commutator-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="mat:block-bound"></a>
We also use a block version of Cauchy–Schwarz. If $X=(X_1,\ldots,X_m)$ and $S^{-1}=I/s+\alpha XX^t$, then $$\label{mat:block-bound}
 \left\|a+\sum_rX_rL_r\right\|_S^2
 \le s\|a\|^2+\frac1\alpha\|L\|^2.$$ This follows column by column because the block operator $S^{1/2}(I/\sqrt{s},\sqrt{\alpha}X)$ has product with its transpose equal to $I$.

<a id="mat:completion-constants"></a>
<a id="mat:weighted-completion"></a>
The scalar estimates in Lemma <a href="#sc:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:bounds">14</a> give $p(a)>0$ for every real $a$. To reserve part of the negative $H$ term for the cost of $\log\det S$, fix $$\label{mat:completion-constants}
 \epsilon=\frac3{20},\qquad\kappa=\frac{49}{100},$$ and put $$\rho=\epsilon I+\kappa S/s.$$ Then $I-\rho>0$. A weighted completion of the square gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \tau\left[T\left(Yh_0+\sum_rX_rM_r\right)\right]
 &\le\tau\bigl(H(I-\rho)p\bigr)
       +\frac{\|M p^{-1/2}\|^2}{4(1-\epsilon)}
       +\frac{\alpha s\|Yh_0p^{-1/2}\|^2}
                    {4(1-\epsilon-\kappa)}.
 \label{mat:weighted-completion}
\end{aligned}$$ To verify that the order of the weights is valid, write $Q_0=H(I-\rho)$ and $F=Yh_0+\sum_rX_rM_r$. Apply $\langle a,b\rangle\le\|a\|^2+\frac14\|b\|^2$ to $Q_0^{1/2}p^{1/2}$ and $Q_0^{-1/2}TFp^{-1/2}$. The resulting second square has left weight bounded above by $$T^2Q_0^{-1}\le\alpha S(I-\rho)^{-1},$$ with equality on the range of $H$. Here inverses are interpreted as pseudoinverses on the common nullspace of $Q_0$ and $T$. Since $$(I-\rho)S^{-1}
   =\frac{1-\epsilon-\kappa}{s}I
           +(1-\epsilon)\alpha XX^t,$$ the same block argument as in <a href="#mat:block-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:block-bound">[mat:block-bound]</a> proves <a href="#mat:weighted-completion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:weighted-completion">[mat:weighted-completion]</a>. Thus this step does not require $p$ to commute with $S$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Two scalar estimates and the final chaos bound

The following estimate combines the cost $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(T-\log S)$ with the negative $H$ term left by <a href="#mat:weighted-completion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:weighted-completion">[mat:weighted-completion]</a> and the terms quadratic in $Y$. A second estimate will control the derivative residuals, including their dependence on $Z$, by $\mathcal C$ and the higher-chaos energy. The proof of the first estimate is given in Section <a href="#sc:absorption-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:absorption-proof">4.1</a>. Its strict remainder controls the eigenvalues of $B$ through the weight <a href="#mat:rigidity-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:rigidity-weight">[mat:rigidity-weight]</a>.

<a id="stmt-mat-scalar-absorption"></a>
<div id="mat:scalar-absorption" class="proposition">

<a id="mat:absorption-inequality"></a>
**Proposition 12** (Absorption with a first-chaos remainder). *Let $A\in L^2(\gamma_m;\operatorname{Sym}_m)$ satisfy $\mathbb EA=0$, and define $X_r,Y,B$ by <a href="#mat:chaos" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:chaos">[mat:chaos]</a> and $S,T,H$ by <a href="#mat:dual-matrices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:dual-matrices">[mat:dual-matrices]</a>. With the constants <a href="#mat:dual-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:dual-constants">[mat:dual-constants]</a>, <a href="#mat:constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:constants">[mat:constants]</a>, and <a href="#mat:completion-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:completion-constants">[mat:completion-constants]</a>, the functions <a href="#mat:scalar-functions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:scalar-functions">[mat:scalar-functions]</a>, and $\rho=\epsilon I+\kappa S/s$, one has $$\label{mat:absorption-inequality}
 \begin{split}
 &\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(T-\log S)-\tau(H\rho p)
 +s\tau\left[Y^2\left((b-\beta)^2+
       \frac{\alpha h_0^2}{4(1-\epsilon-\kappa)p}\right)\right]\\
 &\hspace{15mm}\le\frac{69}{500}\|Y\|^2
       -\frac1{1000}\sum_{i=1}^m\omega(\lambda_i),
 \end{split}$$ where $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_m$ are the eigenvalues of $B$. All scalar factors in the expected traces are evaluated at $A$.*

</div>

The remaining derivative terms are compared entrywise in an eigenbasis of $A$. This comparison retains a multiple of the eigenvalue-separation surplus $\mathcal C$ rather than discarding it.

<a id="stmt-mat-scalar-pair"></a>
<div id="mat:scalar-pair" class="proposition">

<a id="mat:pair-inequality"></a>
**Proposition 13** (Two-eigenvalue estimate). *Let $a_i,a_j\in\mathbb R$ and $G,g,Z\in\mathbb R$. Set $$\delta=\bigl|\log(q(a_i)/q(a_j))\bigr|,\qquad
 r_{ij}=\frac{1+m_0(\min(a_i,a_j))}{16}
       \left(1+\frac{\delta^2}{30}+\frac{\delta^4}{1680}\right),$$ where $m_0$ is the six-interval lower bound in <a href="#tr:bins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:bins">[tr:bins]</a>. Define the endpoint residuals $$\begin{array}{lll}
 D_j=Gb_j+Z\Delta k_{ij},&L_j=Gl_j+Z\Delta b_{ij},&
 M_j=Ge_j+Z\psi_{ij}+cgb_j,\\
 D_i=Gb_i+Z\Delta k_{ji},&L_i=Gl_i+Z\Delta b_{ji},&
 M_i=Ge_i+Z\psi_{ji}+cgb_i.
 \end{array}$$ Let $Q(u,v)=\frac{43}{100}u^2+\frac{26}{100}uv+\frac{68}{25}v^2$. Then $$\begin{aligned}
 &\operatorname{avg}_{v=i,j}\left[
 s(D_v-\beta g)^2+\left(1+\frac s8\right)(D_v-\gamma g)^2
 +\gamma^2(s-1)g^2+\frac{L_v^2}{\alpha}
 +\frac{M_v^2}{4(1-\epsilon)p_v}\right]\notag\\
 &\hspace{15mm}\le Q(G,g-G/2)+r_{ij}\delta^2Z^2.
 \label{mat:pair-inequality}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\operatorname{avg}_{v=i,j}$ is the arithmetic mean of the two endpoint expressions, counting both also when $i=j$. Moreover $p_v=p(a_v)>0$, and all divided differences extend continuously to $a_i=a_j$.*

</div>

The proof of Proposition <a href="#mat:scalar-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:scalar-pair">13</a> occupies Section <a href="#sc:pair-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:pair-proof">4.2</a>. We now show that these two scalar estimates close the matrix argument.

<details id="proof-stmt-mat-scalar-pair"><summary>Proof of stmt-mat-scalar-pair</summary>

<a id="mat:pre-pair"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#mat:entropy-smooth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:entropy-smooth">[mat:entropy-smooth]</a>.* Apply Lemma <a href="#mat:expansion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:expansion">10</a> in <a href="#mat:deficit-reduction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:deficit-reduction">[mat:deficit-reduction]</a>. Bound its last line by Lemma <a href="#mat:commutator" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:commutator">11</a>, its first square by <a href="#mat:block-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:block-bound">[mat:block-bound]</a>, and its linear $T$ term by <a href="#mat:weighted-completion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:weighted-completion">[mat:weighted-completion]</a>. The $H$ terms combine to $-\tau(H\rho p)$. Since $Y=Y^t$ and $h_0,p,b$ are commuting functions of $A$, the two terms quadratic in $Y$ have exactly the form appearing in <a href="#mat:absorption-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:absorption-inequality">[mat:absorption-inequality]</a>, by cyclicity of the trace; no commutation with $Y$ is required. Set $\Omega_B=\sum_{i=1}^m\omega(\lambda_i)$. Consequently, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal{D}\le {}&-V_0-\mathcal C+\frac{69}{500}\|Y\|^2
    -\frac{\Omega_B}{1000}
    +\|D-\beta g\|_S^2
    +\left(1+\frac s8\right)\|D-\gamma g\|^2
    +\frac1\alpha\|L\|^2\nonumber\\
 &\hspace{22mm}+\gamma^2\tau\left(T\sum_rg_r^2\right)
    +\frac{\|Mp^{-1/2}\|^2}{4(1-\epsilon)}.
 \label{mat:pre-pair}
\end{aligned}$$ Use $S\le sI$ and $T\le(s-1)I$. In an eigenbasis of $A$, the entries of $G_r,g_r,Z_r$ are symmetric in $i,j$. Pairing the entries $(i,j)$ and $(j,i)$ therefore gives precisely the endpoint average in <a href="#mat:pair-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:pair-inequality">[mat:pair-inequality]</a>; right multiplication by $p^{-1/2}$ gives the denominator $p_j$ for entry $(i,j)$. Proposition <a href="#mat:scalar-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:scalar-pair">13</a> now bounds the last five terms of <a href="#mat:pre-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:pre-pair">[mat:pre-pair]</a> by $$\mathbb E\sum_{i,j,r}Q\bigl((G_r)_{ij},(g_r)_{ij}-(G_r)_{ij}/2\bigr)
       +\mathcal C.$$ The separation surplus is the same $\mathcal C$ as in Proposition <a href="#tr:surplus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:surplus">7</a>: its coefficient at $(i,j)$ is independent of the derivative index $r$, so summing over $r$ is unchanged by rotating that index. All changes of eigenbasis are pointwise; no eigenvectors are differentiated. The separation surplus cancels. Because $Q$ has constant coefficients, its summed quadratic form is invariant under orthonormal changes of basis. We may therefore return to fixed coordinates and apply the Gaussian chaos decomposition.

For a degree-$d$ component of $Y$, where $d\ge2$, the associated components of $g$ and $G$ satisfy $g=G/d$, and $\|G\|^2=d\|Y\|^2$. Different degrees remain orthogonal after differentiation. The required bound follows, degree by degree, from <a id="mat:final-polynomial"></a>$$\label{mat:final-polynomial}
 Q(1,u-1/2)+\frac7{50}u\le1-u,
 \qquad 0\le u\le\frac12.$$ Indeed, the right side minus the left side factors as $\frac1{50}(1-2u)(1+68u)\ge0$. Set $u=1/d$, multiply by $\|G\|^2$, and sum over the chaoses; the sums converge because $Y\in H^1(\gamma_m)$. The summed $Q$ term is at most $V_0-\frac7{50}\|Y\|^2$. Substitution into <a href="#mat:pre-pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:pre-pair">[mat:pre-pair]</a> therefore gives $$\mathcal{D}\le-\frac1{500}\|Y\|^2-\frac{\Omega_B}{1000},$$ which is <a href="#mat:strict-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:strict-remainder">[mat:strict-remainder]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sc:scalar"></a>
### The two scalar estimates

<span id="sc:section" label="sc:section"></span>

The matrix argument has reduced the entropy inequality to two estimates. Proposition <a href="#mat:scalar-absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:scalar-absorption">12</a> controls the part depending on the first Gaussian chaos; Proposition <a href="#mat:scalar-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:scalar-pair">13</a> controls the remaining quadratic form, one pair of eigenvalues at a time. We prove them here. The functions $q,d,k,b,l$ and the six intervals $O,P_1,R_1,U_1,V_1,T_1$ are those of the transport section, and the constants $\alpha,s,\beta,c,\gamma,\epsilon,\kappa$ and functions $p,e,h_0$ are those of the matrix section. All finite decimals below denote exact rational numbers.

We first state the one-variable bounds used in both proofs. Their verification is given in Appendix <a href="#cert:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:verification">8</a>; in particular, none of the bounds in this section is inferred from numerical sampling. For the quadratic form in Proposition <a href="#mat:scalar-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:scalar-pair">13</a>, set <a id="sc:auxiliary-functions"></a>$$\label{sc:auxiliary-functions}
 \begin{gathered}
 D_*=\frac{1}{4(1-\epsilon)},\qquad
 \mathsf D=\frac{D_*}{p},\quad \mathsf L=\frac{l}{\alpha},\quad
 \mathsf E=e+\gamma b
       =p+b(.75-b)-\frac{l^2}{\alpha},\\
 K_0=\mathsf D\mathsf E,\qquad J_0=\mathsf D cb,\\
 A_*=s+1+s/8,\quad
 \eta_*=s\beta+(1+s/8)\gamma,\quad
 \nu_*=s\beta^2+(9s/8)\gamma^2.
 \end{gathered}$$ The symbols $\mathsf D,\mathsf L,\mathsf E$ denote scalar functions, to be distinguished from the matrix residuals $D_r,L_r,M_r$ of the preceding section.

<a id="stmt-sc-bounds"></a>
<div id="sc:bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="sc:p-h-bounds"></a>
<a id="sc:interval-table"></a>
<a id="sc:local-surplus"></a>
<a id="sc:derivative-table"></a>
**Lemma 14** (One-variable bounds). *For every $a\in\mathbb R$, $$\label{sc:p-h-bounds}
 \begin{gathered}
 p(a)>0,\qquad |h_0(a)|\le .67\sqrt{p(a)},\\
 p(a)\ge\begin{cases}(7+a^2)^{-1},&a\le0,\\ .25,&a\ge0,\end{cases}
 \qquad p(a)\ge .46\quad(a\ge4).
 \end{gathered}$$ The following interval bounds hold: $$\label{sc:interval-table}
\begin{array}{c|cccccc}
 &b&\mathsf L\le&\mathsf D\le&K_0&J_0\le&\ell_1\\\hline
 O &[0,.09]&.60&.295(7+a^2)&[.37,.56]&.72&.13\\
 P_1 &[.083,.15]&1.01&4.3&[.29,.46]&.72&.13\\
 R_1 &[.14,.23]&1.19&3.00&[.29,.371]&.68&.13\\
 U_1 &[.22,.342]&1.19&1.72&[.29,.398]&.61&.123\\
 V_1 &[.337,.45]&.83&.87&[.31,.4]&.50&.060\\
 T_1 &[.445,.5]&.20&.640&[.32,.4]&.48&.022
\end{array}$$ Here $\ell_1$ is the positive number in the last column, and $$\label{sc:local-surplus}
 Q-
 \begin{pmatrix}
 A_*b^2-\eta_*b+\nu_*/4+l^2/\alpha&\nu_*/2-\eta_*b\\
 \nu_*/2-\eta_*b&\nu_*
 \end{pmatrix}
 -\mathsf D
 \begin{pmatrix}\mathsf E\\cb\end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}\mathsf E&cb\end{pmatrix}
 \succeq\begin{pmatrix}\ell_1&0\\0&.75\end{pmatrix}.$$ Here $Q=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}.43&.13\\.13&2.72\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, as in Proposition <a href="#mat:scalar-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:scalar-pair">13</a>. Finally, writing <a id="sc:derivative-functions"></a>$$\label{sc:derivative-functions}
 z=\frac{l'}{l},\qquad h_*=1+3b+kz,\qquad \chi=\frac{l}{d},$$ we have $$\label{sc:derivative-table}
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
 &z&h_*&\chi\le\\\hline
 a\le-1&[-.08,.70]&[.8,1.77]&.070\\
 a\ge-1&[-.475,0]&[.8,1.77]&.097
\end{array}$$ At an endpoint shared by two intervals, either applicable row may be used.*

</div>

The first line of the lemma supplies a lower bound for the mean of $p$ from only a second moment. The matrix surplus in <a href="#sc:local-surplus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:local-surplus">[sc:local-surplus]</a> then provides room to absorb the secant errors in the second proposition. We treat these two uses separately.

<a id="sc:absorption-proof"></a>
#### Absorption using a second moment

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-bounds</summary>

<a id="sc:y-cost"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#mat:scalar-absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:scalar-absorption">12</a>.* By $0<b<1/2$ and <a href="#sc:p-h-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:p-h-bounds">[sc:p-h-bounds]</a>, $$\label{sc:y-cost}
 s\left((b-\beta)^2+
 \frac{\alpha h_0^2}{4(1-\epsilon-\kappa)p}\right)
 \le s\left(.28^2+\frac{.075\cdot .67^2}{4\cdot .36}\right)<.111.$$ It remains to bound the terms involving $T-\log S$ while retaining a negative multiple of the weight $\omega$ in <a href="#mat:rigidity-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="mat:rigidity-weight">[mat:rigidity-weight]</a>.

<a id="sc:spectral-moments"></a>
Fix a unit eigenvector $v$ of $B$ with eigenvalue $\lambda\ge0$. The corresponding eigenvalues of $S,T,H,\rho$ are $$S=(1/s+\alpha\lambda)^{-1},\quad T=S-1,\quad
 H=\alpha S(\lambda-1)^2,\quad \rho=\epsilon+\kappa S/s.$$ Let $\nu_v$ be the probability measure obtained by averaging the spectral measure of $A$ at $v$. The decomposition of $A$ into its first chaos and $Y$ gives $$\label{sc:spectral-moments}
 \int a\,d\nu_v(a)=0,\qquad
 \int a^2\,d\nu_v(a)=\lambda+r,
 \qquad r=v^{\mathsf T}\mathbb E(Y^2)v\ge0.$$ Indeed, orthogonality of the Gaussian chaoses gives $\mathbb EA^2=B+\mathbb EY^2$, including the vanishing of both matrix cross terms.

<a id="sc:p-minorant"></a>
For $0<u\le.13$, define $$n_u=\frac{u^{3/2}}{2},\qquad
 d_u=\frac{u^3}{16(.46-u)}.$$ We claim the following quadratic minorant: $$\label{sc:p-minorant}
 p(a)\ge u+n_ua-d_ua^2\qquad(a\in\mathbb R).$$ For $a\le0$, put $x=-\sqrt u\,a$. If $x\ge2$, the right side is nonpositive. If $0\le x\le2$, it is enough to use $p(a)\ge u/(x^2+7u)$ and $$1-(1-x/2)(x^2+7u)
 \ge \frac{x(x-1)^2}{2}+.045(2-x)\ge0.$$ For $0\le a\le4$, the right side of <a href="#sc:p-minorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:p-minorant">[sc:p-minorant]</a> is at most $.13+2(.13)^{3/2}<.25$. For $a\ge4$, its maximum over all real $a$ is $u+n_u^2/(4d_u)=.46$. Thus <a href="#sc:p-minorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:p-minorant">[sc:p-minorant]</a> follows from <a href="#sc:p-h-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:p-h-bounds">[sc:p-h-bounds]</a> in all three ranges.

<a id="sc:p-mean"></a>
Choose $$u=\min(.13,1.28/\sqrt\lambda),$$ with $u=.13$ when $\lambda=0$. Since $\lambda u^2\le1.28^2$, integration of the minorant against <a href="#sc:spectral-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:spectral-moments">[sc:spectral-moments]</a> yields $$\label{sc:p-mean}
 \int p\,d\nu_v\ge u-d_u(\lambda+r)
 \ge .689u-d_ur.$$ The second inequality uses $1-1.28^2/[16(.46-.13)]>.689$. We also have <a id="sc:rho-loss"></a>$$\label{sc:rho-loss}
 H\rho d_u\le .027.$$ To check this, note that $\rho\le.64$ and that $H\le\lambda$ for $\lambda\ge1$, whereas $H\le\alpha s$ for $\lambda\le1$. In the former case, $$H\rho d_u\le
 \frac{.64\cdot1.28^2\cdot.13}{16(.46-.13)}<.02582;$$ in the latter, $\alpha s\cdot.64\cdot .13^3/[16(.46-.13)]<.027$.

<a id="sc:log-bound"></a>
For completeness, we next verify the elementary logarithmic bound needed to compare $T-\log S$ with the positive term in <a href="#sc:p-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:p-mean">[sc:p-mean]</a>. Set $$F_0(S)=\frac{S(S-1-\log S)}{(1-S)^2},\qquad F_0(1)=\tfrac12.$$ For $0<S\le s$ and $\lambda=(1/S-1/s)/\alpha$, we claim $$\label{sc:log-bound}
 \frac{\alpha F_0(S)}{\epsilon+\kappa S/s}
 \le\min(.089,.88/\sqrt\lambda)\le .688u,$$ where the second bound in the minimum is interpreted as $+\infty$ at $\lambda=0$. First, $$F_0(S)=\int_0^1 \frac{Sx}{1-x+xS}\,dx$$ is concave. Its tangent at $S=.28$ has intercept below $.16$ and slope below $.52$, so $F_0(S)\le .16+.52S$. These two strict inequalities can, for example, be checked from $$-\log(.28)=2\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(9/16)^{2j+1}}{2j+1},$$ using twelve terms and the geometric bound for the remainder. Coefficient comparison now gives $$.075(.16+.52S)<.089(.15+.45325S),$$ which proves the first bound in the minimum.

<a id="sc:F0-sqrt"></a>
For the second bound we use $$\label{sc:F0-sqrt}
 \frac{F_0(S)}{\sqrt S}\le .478+1.4S\qquad(S>0).$$ Here is a direct verification, including the point $S=1$ by continuity. For $x>0$, let $$J(x)=\frac{(.478+1.4x^2)(1-x^2)^2}{x}
       -(x^2-1-2\log x).$$ Then $$x^2J'(x)=P(x):=-.478+2x+.444x^2-2x^3-6.966x^4+7x^6.$$ Descartes’ rule of signs gives at most three positive roots of $P$, counting multiplicity. The signs at $.24,.26,.6$, together with $P(1)=0$ and $P'(1)>0$, give exactly three: one in $(.24,.26)$, one in $(.26,.6)$, and $1$. Thus the only local minima of $J$ occur at the first root and at $1$. We have $J(1)=0$ and $J(.25)>.14$; the latter follows already from $\log2<.694$. On $[.24,.26]$, $|P(.25)|<.01$ and $|P'|<4$, hence $|J'|<2$. The first minimum is therefore positive. Also $J(x)\to+\infty$ at both ends of $(0,\infty)$. This proves $J\ge0$, which is equivalent to <a href="#sc:F0-sqrt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:F0-sqrt">[sc:F0-sqrt]</a> away from $x=1$.

Using $\alpha\lambda=1/S-1/s$, inequality <a href="#sc:F0-sqrt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:F0-sqrt">[sc:F0-sqrt]</a> gives $$\frac{\alpha F_0(S)\sqrt\lambda}{\rho}
 \le \sqrt\alpha\sqrt{1-S/s}\,
       \frac{.478+1.4S}{.15+.45325S}
 \le \frac{\sqrt{.075}\cdot .478}{.15}<.88.$$ The last ratio decreases in $S$ because $1.4\cdot.15<.478\cdot.45325$. Finally, $.089<.688\cdot.13$ and $.88<.688\cdot1.28$ prove the last inequality in <a href="#sc:log-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:log-bound">[sc:log-bound]</a>.

For $\lambda\ne1$ we have $$\frac{T-\log S}{H\rho}=\frac{\alpha F_0(S)}{\rho}.$$ Combining <a href="#sc:p-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:p-mean">[sc:p-mean]</a>–<a href="#sc:log-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:log-bound">[sc:log-bound]</a>, and treating $\lambda=1$ by continuity, gives $$T-\log S-H\rho\int p\,d\nu_v
       \le -.001H\rho u+.027r.$$ The definitions give $H\rho u=\omega(\lambda)$. Sum over an orthonormal eigenbasis of the matrix $B$, and add <a href="#sc:y-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:y-cost">[sc:y-cost]</a>. This summation evaluates $\tau(H\rho p(A))$ without requiring $p(A)$ to commute with $B$. Likewise, <a href="#sc:y-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:y-cost">[sc:y-cost]</a> may be integrated against the positive semidefinite matrix $Y^2$ without a commutation assumption. Since $\sum_v r=\lVert Y\rVert^2$, the result is $$(.027+.111)\lVert Y\rVert^2-.001\sum_i\omega(\lambda_i)
 =\frac{69}{500}\lVert Y\rVert^2-\frac1{1000}\sum_i\omega(\lambda_i),$$ as asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="sc:pair-proof"></a>
#### A quadratic estimate for two endpoints

<details id="proof-stmt-sc-bounds-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-sc-bounds</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#mat:scalar-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:scalar-pair">13</a>.* Fix two endpoints $a_-\le a_+$ and write $$\delta=\log q(a_+)-\log q(a_-),\qquad m_*=m_0(a_-).$$ When the endpoints coincide, all secant increments vanish, and <a href="#sc:local-surplus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:local-surplus">[sc:local-surplus]</a> immediately proves the assertion. We henceforth suppose $\delta>0$.

<a id="sc:increment-norm"></a>
<a id="sc:one-endpoint"></a>
We first isolate the cost of the secant increments at one endpoint $a_j$. Use coordinates $(G,h)$ with $h=g-G/2$. With $Z=0$, the left side of the endpoint quadratic form in Proposition <a href="#mat:scalar-pair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:scalar-pair">13</a> is exactly the two matrices subtracted from $Q$ in <a href="#sc:local-surplus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:local-surplus">[sc:local-surplus]</a>. For an increment vector $(x,y,w)$, define $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal N_j(x,y,w)^2={}&A_*x^2+y^2/\alpha+\mathsf D_jw^2\notag\\
 &+\frac{((A_*b_j-\eta_*/2)x+\mathsf L_jy+K_{0j}w)^2}{\ell_{1j}}
   +\frac{(-\eta_*x+J_{0j}w)^2}{.75}.
 \label{sc:increment-norm}
\end{aligned}$$ Completing squares against the surplus $\ell_{1j}G^2+.75h^2$ in <a href="#sc:local-surplus" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:local-surplus">[sc:local-surplus]</a> shows that the full endpoint form is bounded above by $$\label{sc:one-endpoint}
 Q(G,h)+Z^2\mathcal N_j(\Delta k,\Delta b,\psi_j)^2.$$ Indeed, the two coefficients of $2GZ$ and $2hZ$ are precisely the numerators of the two squared terms in <a href="#sc:increment-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:increment-norm">[sc:increment-norm]</a>. The first line of that formula is the coefficient of $Z^2$ before completion of squares.

The next step estimates these increment norms by integrating derivatives between the endpoints. Recall that a secant slope is the uniform average of the derivative over $[a_-,a_+]$. With $\xi=\log q(a)$, differentiation therefore gives the vector <a id="sc:derivative-vector"></a>$$\label{sc:derivative-vector}
 v_j(a)=\chi(a)(1,z(a),W_j(a)),\qquad
 W_j(a)=h_*(a)-2b_j-2\mathsf L_jz(a).$$ More explicitly, $(\Delta k,\Delta b,\psi_j)$ is the uniform average in $a$ of $\int_{\log q(a_j)}^{\log q(a)}v_j(q^{-1}(e^\xi))\,d\xi$. This follows from $b'=l$, $l'=zl$, $d(\log q)/da=d$, and $f''=l(1+3b+kz)=lh_*$. Put $$F_j(a)=\mathcal N_j(v_j(a))^2.$$

<a id="sc:F-simple"></a>
<a id="sc:F-negative"></a>
We record the bounds furnished by Lemma <a href="#sc:bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sc:bounds">14</a>. If the endpoint $a_j$ belongs to $R_1,U_1,V_1,T_1$, respectively, then $$\label{sc:F-simple}
 F_j(a)\le .295,\quad .23,\quad .23,\quad .39
 \qquad\hbox{for all }a.$$ For $a\le-1$, the $R_1$ bound improves to $.20$. For $a_j\in P_1$, the bounds are $.42^2$ when $a\le-1$ and $.61^2$ when $a\ge-1$. If $a_j\in O$, write $t_j=-a_j$; then $$\label{sc:F-negative}
 \frac{F_j(a)}{\chi(a)^2}\le
 \begin{cases}
 20+1.04(7+t_j^2),&a\le-1,\\
 12+1.63(7+t_j^2),&a\ge-1.
 \end{cases}$$ For clarity, the complete finite arithmetic behind these bounds is given by the following table. Its three entries bound, in order, $$|W_j|,\qquad
 |A_*b_j-\eta_*/2+\mathsf L_jz+K_{0j}W_j|,\qquad
 |-\eta_*+J_{0j}W_j|.$$ <a id="sc:substitution-table"></a>$$\label{sc:substitution-table}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
 &a\le-1&a\ge-1\\\hline
 O&(1.867,.52,1.314)&(2.34,.55,1.155)\\
 P_1&(1.766,.49,1.813)&(2.564,.433,1.155)\\
 R_1&(1.681,.664,2.057)&(2.621,.420,1.155)\\
 U_1&(1.55,.847,2.101)&(2.461,.615,1.155)\\
 V_1&(1.263,.912,1.787)&(1.885,.770,1.205)\\
 T_1&(.913,.869,1.386)&(1.071,.840,1.251)
\end{array}$$ To verify it, substitute the intervals in <a href="#sc:interval-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:interval-table">[sc:interval-table]</a> and <a href="#sc:derivative-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:derivative-table">[sc:derivative-table]</a>; each expression is affine in each of its variables separately, so its extrema occur at corners of the corresponding box. For the middle expression, first write it as $$(A_*-2K_{0j})b_j-\eta_*/2+K_{0j}h_*+
 (1-2K_{0j})\mathsf L_jz.$$ Substituting these three bounds into <a href="#sc:increment-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:increment-norm">[sc:increment-norm]</a>, together with the bounds for $\mathsf D_j,\ell_{1j},z,\chi$, proves <a href="#sc:F-simple" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:F-simple">[sc:F-simple]</a>–<a href="#sc:F-negative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:F-negative">[sc:F-negative]</a>. All these are finite rational comparisons; the exact verifier accompanying Appendix <a href="#cert:verification" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:verification">8</a> also checks them.

<a id="sc:mean-distance"></a>
It remains to compare the integrated derivative cost with the logarithmic separation surplus. Let $u_-$ be the mean of $(\log q(a)-\log q(a_-))/\delta$ under the uniform probability measure on $[a_-,a_+]$, and put $u_+=1-u_-$. In the $\xi$ coordinate this measure has density proportional to $D_0=1/d$. The excess-variance bound in Lemma <a href="#tr:q-properties" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:q-properties">3</a> gives $$0\le \frac{d\log D_0}{d\xi}=\frac{1-qd}{d^2}\le1.$$ The density $D_0$ is increasing, whereas $D_0e^{-\xi}$ is decreasing. The covariance of an increasing function with an increasing function is nonnegative, and with a decreasing function is nonpositive. Applying this observation to the coordinate $\xi$ compares its mean first with constant density and then with density proportional to $e^\xi$. The latter comparison gives $u_-\le\tfrac12+\tfrac12\coth(\delta/2)-1/\delta$, and hence $$\label{sc:mean-distance}
 \tfrac12\le u_-\le\tfrac12+\delta/12,
 \qquad u_-^2+u_+^2\le\tfrac12(1+\delta^2/36).$$ For the upper bound one uses $\coth x-1/x\le x/3$, obtained by comparing the power series of $x\cosh x$ and $(1+x^2/3)\sinh x$.

<a id="sc:threshold"></a>
Suppose $P_-$ and $P_+$ have the following property: on every partial $\xi$-interval starting at the indicated endpoint, the mean of $\mathcal N_j(v_j)$ is at most $\sqrt{P_j}$. Pointwise bounds for $F_j$ are one way to ensure this property. Minkowski’s inequality, followed by the uniform average in $a$, gives $$\mathcal N_-(\Delta k,\Delta b,\psi_-)\le\sqrt{P_-}\,\delta u_-,
 \qquad
 \mathcal N_+(\Delta k,\Delta b,\psi_+)\le\sqrt{P_+}\,\delta u_+.$$ Since $u_-\ge u_+\ge0$, $$P_-u_-^2+P_+u_+^2
 \le\max\left(P_-,\frac{P_-+P_+}{2}\right)(u_-^2+u_+^2).$$ Averaging <a href="#sc:one-endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:one-endpoint">[sc:one-endpoint]</a> and applying <a href="#sc:mean-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:mean-distance">[sc:mean-distance]</a> therefore proves the desired estimate whenever $$\label{sc:threshold}
 \max\left(P_-,\frac{P_-+P_+}{2}\right)\le
 \Theta_{m_*}(\delta):=
 \frac{1+m_*}{4}\,
 \frac{1+\delta^2/30+\delta^4/1680}{1+\delta^2/36}.$$ Indeed, the resulting coefficient of $Z^2$ is at most $\delta^2(1+\delta^2/36)\Theta_{m_*}(\delta)/4
=r_{-+}\delta^2$.

We verify <a href="#sc:threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:threshold">[sc:threshold]</a> for all endpoint positions. The function $\Theta_m$ increases in $\delta\ge0$: differentiation with respect to $y=\delta^2$ leaves a positive numerator $1/180+2y/1680+y^2/(1680\cdot36)$. We will also use the following observation about partial intervals: if a smaller bound holds on an initial segment of logarithmic length $L$, then its fraction in a partial interval of length $h\le\delta$ is at least $\min(1,L/h)\ge L/\delta$, provided $\delta\ge L$. If $a_-\in R_1$, take $P_-\le.295$ and $P_+\le.39$; then $(P_-+P_+)/2\le.3425<\Theta_{.38}(0)=.345$. For $a_-\in U_1$ or $V_1$, use $P_-\le.23$, $P_+\le.39$ and $\Theta_{.68}(0)=.42$. For $a_-\in T_1$, both bounds are $.39<.42$.

Now suppose $a_-\in P_1$. If $a_+\le-1$, both endpoint costs are at most $.20<\Theta_{.19}(0)$, using the $R_1$ row at $a_+=-1$. Otherwise, the part of the logarithmic interval below $-1$ has length at least $1.62$. Hence every partial interval from the lower endpoint has mean norm at most $$.61-\frac{(.61-.42)1.62}{\delta},\qquad
 P_-=(.61-.3078/\delta)^2.$$ The length assertion follows from $q(-1)>.287$, $q(-2)<.056$. If the upper endpoint lies in $T_1$, then $\delta>4$ because $q(4)>4$. For $\delta\le4$, therefore, $P_+\le.23$ and $P_-\le.284143<\Theta_{.19}(0)=.2975$. For $4\le\delta\le5$, use $P_-<.300787$, $P_+\le.39$, and $$\max(.300787,(.300787+.39)/2)<.345394
 <\Theta_{.19}(4).$$ For $\delta\ge5$, use $P_-\le.61^2=.3721$ and $$\max(.3721,(.3721+.39)/2)=.38105<\Theta_{.19}(5).$$ This covers $P_1$.

<a id="sc:O-low-cost"></a>
<a id="sc:theta-tail"></a>
Finally suppose $a_-\in O$. For any endpoint $a_j\in O$ and any argument $a\le-1$ between the endpoints, $$t_j^2\le a^2+2\delta,\qquad |a|\chi(a)\le .09.$$ The first follows by integrating $d\ge-a$ on the negative half-line; the second follows from $l\le .075\cdot1.19<.09$ and $d\ge|a|$. Using $\chi\le.07$ in <a href="#sc:F-negative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:F-negative">[sc:F-negative]</a> gives $$\label{sc:O-low-cost}
 F_j(a)\le .144+.011\delta.$$ If the whole interval lies at or below $-1$, take $P_-=.144+.011\delta$ and $P_+\le\max(P_-,.20)$; the latter bound also covers upper endpoints in $P_1$ and $R_1$. For $0\le\delta\le4$, both are below $.25$. For $\delta\ge4$, use $$\label{sc:theta-tail}
 \Theta_0(\delta)\ge .25+.00260\delta^2.$$ This follows on clearing denominators and using $\delta^2\ge16$. The quadratic $.106-.011\delta+.00260\delta^2$ is strictly positive, so <a href="#sc:threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:threshold">[sc:threshold]</a> follows in this case too.

If $a_+>-1$, the first part of the logarithmic interval has length at least $4$, since $q(-3)<.005$ and $q(-1)>.287$. For $a\ge-1$, the same integration gives $t_-^2\le1+2\delta$; using $\chi\le.097$ in <a href="#sc:F-negative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:F-negative">[sc:F-negative]</a> yields $F_-(a)\le .236+.031\delta$. On every partial interval from the lower endpoint, Jensen’s inequality therefore permits the uniform bound $$P_-=.236+.031\delta-\frac{4}{\delta}(.092+.020\delta)
     =.156+.031\delta-.368/\delta.$$ For partial intervals contained below $-1$, the same bound follows from <a href="#sc:O-low-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:O-low-cost">[sc:O-low-cost]</a>, because $\delta\ge4$. By <a href="#sc:theta-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:theta-tail">[sc:theta-tail]</a>, $$\Theta_0(\delta)-P_-
 \ge .094-.031\delta+.00260\delta^2+.368/\delta>0;$$ the quadratic has negative discriminant and positive leading coefficient. If $a_+\notin T_1$, then $P_+\le.23<\Theta_0(\delta)$. If $a_+\in T_1$, then $\delta>6.5$ from $q(4)>4$ and $q(-3)<.005$, and $P_+\le.39<\Theta_0(6.5)<\Theta_0(\delta)$. This proves <a href="#sc:threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sc:threshold">[sc:threshold]</a> in every case and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="geo:section"></a>
### Approximation of log-concave densities

The smooth estimate of Theorem <a href="#mat:entropy-smooth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:entropy-smooth">[mat:entropy-smooth]</a> requires a potential with positive, bounded Hessian. We remove those restrictions while preserving the entropy, covariance, and first moments. The same approximation will let us study equality by applying the strict remainder estimate to smooth densities whose entropy gaps tend to zero.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Convex approximation with entropy convergence

We use extended-valued convex potentials: $V:\mathbb R^m\to(-\infty,+\infty]$ is proper if it is finite somewhere and never takes the value $-\infty$. The convention $e^{-\infty}=0$ allows bounded supports. The elementary tail estimate below supplies a common integrable bound for the approximation, including its entropy integrand.

<a id="stmt-geo-coercivity"></a>
<div id="geo:coercivity" class="lemma">

<a id="geo:linear-coercivity"></a>
**Lemma 15**. *Let $V:\mathbb R^m\to(-\infty,+\infty]$ be proper, lower semicontinuous, and convex, with $$0<\int_{\mathbb R^m}e^{-V(x)}\,\,\mathrm dx<\infty.$$ Then there are $a>0$ and $b\geq0$ such that $$\label{geo:linear-coercivity}
  V(x)\geq a|x|-b\qquad(x\in\mathbb R^m).$$ In particular, $e^{-V}$ has moments of every order, and $\int |V|e^{-V}<\infty$, where $|V|e^{-V}$ is interpreted as zero when $V=+\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-coercivity"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-coercivity</summary>

*Proof.* The convex set $\{V<\infty\}$ has positive measure and hence nonempty interior: otherwise its affine hull would be a proper affine subspace. Choose a simplex with all vertices in this set and with nonempty interior. Convexity bounds $V$ above by the largest of its values at the vertices throughout the simplex. Consequently, some ball $B(x_0,r)$ lies in a sublevel set $\{V\leq M\}$, where $M\geq V(x_0)$.

The closed convex set $A=\{V\leq M+1\}$ has finite volume, since $$|A|\leq e^{M+1}\int e^{-V}.$$ It is bounded. Indeed, the convex hull of $B(x_0,r)$ and a point $z\in A$ contains a cone with base an $(m-1)$-dimensional disk of radius $r$ through $x_0$, perpendicular to $z-x_0$, and height $|z-x_0|$. Its volume tends to infinity with $|z-x_0|$. In dimension one the same assertion follows from the length of the interval joining $x_0$ to $z$.

Choose $R>r$ so large that $A\subset B(x_0,R)$. If $d=|x-x_0|\geq R$ and $V(x)<\infty$, put $y=x_0+(R/d)(x-x_0)$. Then $y\notin A$, and convexity gives $$M+1<V(y)\leq \left(1-\frac Rd\right)V(x_0)+\frac RdV(x).$$ Thus $V(x)>V(x_0)+d/R$. The inequality is automatic if $V(x)=+\infty$. On the compact ball $\overline B(x_0,R)$, lower semicontinuity and properness give a finite lower bound: a sequence with values tending to $-\infty$ would have a convergent subsequence and violate lower semicontinuity. Combining these two bounds proves <a href="#geo:linear-coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:linear-coercivity">[geo:linear-coercivity]</a>, after enlarging $b$.

<a id="geo:entropy-domination"></a>
The moment assertion follows from $e^{-V(x)}\leq e^b e^{-a|x|}$. For the entropy assertion, the elementary bound $$\label{geo:entropy-domination}
  |u|e^{-u}\leq C_b e^{-u/2}\qquad(u\geq-b)$$ holds because $|u|e^{-u/2}$ is bounded on $[-b,\infty)$. Substitute $u=V(x)$ and use <a href="#geo:linear-coercivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:linear-coercivity">[geo:linear-coercivity]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-geo-approximation"></a>
<div id="geo:approximation" class="lemma">

<a id="geo:hessian-bounds"></a>
<a id="geo:approximation-limits"></a>
**Lemma 16**. *Let $f=e^{-V}$ be a log-concave probability density on $\mathbb R^m$, represented by a proper lower semicontinuous convex potential $V$. There are smooth convex potentials $V_j$ and probability densities $$f_j=Z_j^{-1}e^{-V_j},\qquad Z_j=\int_{\mathbb R^m}e^{-V_j},$$ such that $$\label{geo:hessian-bounds}
  \frac2jI\preceq D^2V_j\preceq\left(j+\frac2j\right)I
  \qquad(j\geq1),$$ and $$\label{geo:approximation-limits}
  Z_j\longrightarrow1,\qquad \lVert f_j-f\rVert_{L^1}\longrightarrow0,\qquad
  h(f_j)\longrightarrow h(f),\qquad
  \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f_j)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f).$$ The covariance limit is entrywise, the means of $f_j$ converge to the mean of $f$, and all these quantities are finite.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-approximation"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-approximation</summary>

<a id="geo:moreau"></a>
<a id="geo:envelope-lower"></a>
*Proof.* Fix $a,b$ from Lemma <a href="#geo:coercivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:coercivity">15</a>. For each positive integer $j$, define the Moreau envelope (Moreau 1965, sec. 7) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Moreau1965](#ref-Moreau1965)] $$\label{geo:moreau}
  Q_j(x)=\inf_{y\in\mathbb R^m}\left\{V(y)+\frac j2|x-y|^2\right\}.$$ The expression in braces is lower semicontinuous, strictly convex on its effective domain, and tends to infinity as $|y|\to\infty$. It therefore has a unique minimizer $p_j(x)$, and $Q_j$ is finite everywhere. Moreover, $$\label{geo:envelope-lower}
  Q_j(x)\geq a|x|-b-\frac{a^2}{2j}.$$ Indeed, $V(y)\geq a|x|-a|x-y|-b$, and minimizing $jr^2/2-ar$ over $r\geq0$ proves the bound.

For completeness, the needed regularity of $Q_j$ follows directly from the minimization. The optimality condition is $j(x-p_j(x))\in\partial V(p_j(x))$, where $\partial V$ denotes the convex subdifferential. To obtain it, compare the minimum at $p=p_j(x)$ with $p+s(z-p)$, use convexity to bound $V(p+s(z-p))$ above, divide by $s>0$, and let $s\downarrow0$. The resulting inequality is $V(z)\geq V(p)+j\langle x-p,z-p\rangle$. Adding the two subgradient inequalities at $p_j(x)$ and $p_j(x')$ gives $$\langle x-x',p_j(x)-p_j(x')\rangle
  \geq |p_j(x)-p_j(x')|^2.$$ Consequently both $p_j$ and $x\mapsto x-p_j(x)$ are $1$-Lipschitz. Evaluating the infimum at $p_j(x)$ and, in the reverse direction, at $p_j(x+h)$ shows that $$\nabla Q_j(x)=j(x-p_j(x)).$$ For example, the first comparison gives an upper error $j|h|^2/2$ after subtracting $j\langle x-p_j(x),h\rangle$, and the second gives a lower error of order $j|h|^2$ by the Lipschitz estimate just proved. Thus $Q_j$ is continuously differentiable with $j$-Lipschitz gradient. It is convex, since the function minimized in <a href="#geo:moreau" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:moreau">[geo:moreau]</a> is jointly convex in $(x,y)$.

<a id="geo:envelope-limit"></a>
We next check convergence before smoothing. The functions $Q_j$ increase with $j$, and $Q_j(x)\leq V(x)$ when $V(x)$ is finite. If $Q_j(x)$ stays bounded above as $j\to\infty$, the identity at the minimizer and $V\geq-b$ imply $$|x-p_j(x)|^2\leq\frac{2(Q_j(x)+b)}j\longrightarrow0.$$ Lower semicontinuity then gives $V(x)\leq\liminf_j V(p_j(x))\leq\lim_jQ_j(x)$. This proves $$\label{geo:envelope-limit}
  Q_j(x)\longrightarrow V(x)\quad\hbox{for every }x,$$ including the value $+\infty$.

<a id="geo:common-tail"></a>
Choose a nonnegative even smooth function $\eta$ supported in the unit ball with integral one, and set $\eta_\varepsilon(x)=\varepsilon^{-m}
\eta(x/\varepsilon)$. Define <a id="geo:smooth-potentials"></a>$$\label{geo:smooth-potentials}
  V_j=Q_j*\eta_{1/j}+\frac{|x|^2}j.$$ Convolution preserves convexity and the Lipschitz bound on the gradient; hence <a href="#geo:hessian-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:hessian-bounds">[geo:hessian-bounds]</a> holds. Evenness gives $\int z\eta(z)\,\,\mathrm dz=0$, so Jensen’s inequality and <a href="#geo:envelope-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:envelope-lower">[geo:envelope-lower]</a> yield the common bound $$\label{geo:common-tail}
  V_j(x)\geq Q_j(x)\geq a|x|-B,
  \qquad B=b+\frac{a^2}2.$$

Smoothing at this scale does not affect the limit. The $j$-Lipschitz bound on the gradient gives $$Q_j(x-z)\leq Q_j(x)-\langle\nabla Q_j(x),z\rangle+\frac j2|z|^2.$$ Integrating against $\eta_{1/j}$ cancels the linear term, so $$0\leq (Q_j*\eta_{1/j})(x)-Q_j(x)\leq\frac1{2j}
  \qquad(x\in\mathbb R^m).$$ Together with <a href="#geo:envelope-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:envelope-limit">[geo:envelope-limit]</a>, this proves $V_j(x)\to V(x)$ everywhere.

Now <a href="#geo:common-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:common-tail">[geo:common-tail]</a> dominates $(1+|x|^2)e^{-V_j(x)}$ by an integrable function independent of $j$. Applying <a href="#geo:entropy-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:entropy-domination">[geo:entropy-domination]</a> with $B$ in place of $b$ also gives $$|V_j(x)|e^{-V_j(x)}\leq C e^{-a|x|/2}.$$ At points where $V=+\infty$, both $e^{-V_j}$ and $V_je^{-V_j}$ tend to zero. Dominated convergence therefore proves convergence of the normalizing constants, first moments, second moments, and the integrals $\int V_je^{-V_j}$. Finally, $$h(f_j)=\frac1{Z_j}\int V_je^{-V_j}+\log Z_j,$$ which proves all of <a href="#geo:approximation-limits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:approximation-limits">[geo:approximation-limits]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-approximation-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-approximation</summary>

*Proof of the inequality in Theorem <a href="#intro:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:entropy">2</a>.* Apply Theorem <a href="#mat:entropy-smooth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:entropy-smooth">[mat:entropy-smooth]</a> to the normalized potentials $V_j+\log Z_j$ of Lemma <a href="#geo:approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:approximation">16</a>. Their Hessians satisfy <a href="#geo:hessian-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:hessian-bounds">[geo:hessian-bounds]</a>, so $$h(f_j)\geq m+\frac12\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f_j).$$ The covariance of $f$ is positive definite: zero variance in a nonzero direction would concentrate this Lebesgue density on an affine hyperplane. Thus <a href="#geo:approximation-limits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:approximation-limits">[geo:approximation-limits]</a> permits passage to the limit in both sides and proves the inequality. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:rigidity"></a>
### From vanishing slack to exponential products

Write $\delta(f)=h(f)-m-\tfrac12\log\det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f)$ for the nonnegative entropy gap. Theorem <a href="#mat:entropy-smooth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:entropy-smooth">[mat:entropy-smooth]</a> controls the nonlinear part of the normalized matrix field and the sum of squares of its linear coefficients. We now show that this control determines every equality case. The structural step is local: the first two nonconstant terms of $q(A(x))$ force the coefficients of a linear symmetric matrix field $A$ to commute whenever $q(A)$ is a Jacobian.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Compatibility of a nonlinear Jacobian

<a id="stmt-lem-linear-jacobian"></a>
<div id="lem:linear-jacobian" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:rigid-map"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Rigidity of a linear matrix field). *Let $m\ge1$, and let $X_1,\ldots,X_m$ be real symmetric $m\times m$ matrices satisfying $$\sum_{r=1}^m X_r^2=I,
  \qquad A(x)=\sum_{r=1}^m x_rX_r.$$ Suppose $F\in W^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^m;\mathbb R^m)$ has weak Jacobian $DF=q(A)$ almost everywhere. Then there are an orthogonal matrix $U$, signs $\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_m\in\{-1,1\}$, and $w\in\mathbb R^m$ such that $$\label{eq:rigid-map}
  U^{\mathsf T}F(Uy)
  =\bigl(\sigma_i t(\sigma_i y_i)\bigr)_{i=1}^m+w
  \qquad\text{for almost every }y\in\mathbb R^m.$$ In particular, $F_\#\gamma_m$ is an affine image of the product of $m$ mean-one exponential laws, and its covariance matrix is $I$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-linear-jacobian"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-linear-jacobian</summary>

<a id="eq:jacobian-compatibility"></a>
<a id="eq:coefficient-symmetry"></a>
*Proof.* Write $e_1,\ldots,e_m$ for the standard basis and $J=q(A)$. Commutation of distributional derivatives of $F$ gives $$\label{eq:jacobian-compatibility}
  (\partial_rJ)e_s=(\partial_sJ)e_r
  \qquad(1\le r,s\le m).$$ The scalar function $q$ is real analytic near zero. Since $A(0)=0$, its convergent power series, interpreted by matrix multiplication, gives $$J(x)=q(0)I+q'(0)A(x)+\tfrac12q''(0)A(x)^2+O(|x|^3)$$ near zero; the differentiated remainder is $O(|x|^2)$. The identity $q'(a)=q(a)(q(a)-a)$ yields $$q(0)=\sqrt{\frac2\pi},\qquad
  q'(0)=\frac2\pi>0,\qquad
  q''(0)=\sqrt{\frac2\pi}\left(\frac4\pi-1\right)>0.$$ Thus <a href="#eq:jacobian-compatibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jacobian-compatibility">[eq:jacobian-compatibility]</a>, which is a pointwise identity between analytic functions near zero, has constant term $$\label{eq:coefficient-symmetry}
  X_re_s=X_se_r.$$ Equivalently, $A(x)z=A(z)x$ for all $x,z\in\mathbb R^m$; in particular, $A(x)e_s=X_sx$. Its degree-one term is $$(X_rA(x)+A(x)X_r)e_s
  =(X_sA(x)+A(x)X_s)e_r.$$ The terms with leftmost factor $A(x)$ cancel by <a href="#eq:coefficient-symmetry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coefficient-symmetry">[eq:coefficient-symmetry]</a>. The remaining equality is $(X_rX_s-X_sX_r)x=0$ near zero, and hence <a id="eq:coefficients-commute"></a>$$\label{eq:coefficients-commute}
  X_rX_s=X_sX_r\qquad(1\le r,s\le m).$$

Commuting real symmetric matrices admit a common orthonormal eigenbasis. Choose the orthogonal matrix $U$ with those basis vectors as columns, and rotate both the input and the output. Then $$\widetilde A(y)=U^{\mathsf T}A(Uy)U
     =\sum_{i=1}^m y_i\widetilde X_i,
  \qquad
  \widetilde X_i=U^{\mathsf T}
      \left(\sum_{r=1}^m U_{ri}X_r\right)U$$ has diagonal coefficients. Orthogonality of $U$ gives $\sum_i\widetilde X_i^2=I$, and the identity $A(x)z=A(z)x$ gives $\widetilde X_i e_j=\widetilde X_j e_i$. If $i\ne j$, these two vectors are multiples of different basis vectors, so both are zero. Consequently, only the $i$th diagonal entry of $\widetilde X_i$ can be nonzero. The sum-of-squares identity makes that entry a sign: $$\widetilde X_i=\sigma_i e_ie_i^{\mathsf T},
  \qquad
  \widetilde A(y)=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_1y_1,\ldots,\sigma_my_m).$$ It follows that $$D_y\bigl(U^{\mathsf T}F(Uy)\bigr)
   =\operatorname{diag}\bigl(q(\sigma_1y_1),\ldots,q(\sigma_my_m)\bigr).$$ Since $t'=q$ and $\sigma_i^2=1$, the right side is also the Jacobian of $y\mapsto(\sigma_i t(\sigma_i y_i))_{i=1}^m$. Their difference has zero weak gradient on the connected set $\mathbb R^m$ and is therefore almost everywhere constant. This proves <a href="#eq:rigid-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rigid-map">[eq:rigid-map]</a>.

For independent standard Gaussian coordinates $G_i$, the variables $t(\sigma_iG_i)$ are independent mean-one exponentials. Their variances are one, and the signs and the orthogonal matrix preserve covariance $I$. This proves the final assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Compactness and the proof of the equality classification

We first record the functional-calculus estimate that turns convergence of the normalized fields into convergence of transport Jacobians. The Frobenius norm is essential to this elementary argument.

<a id="stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz"></a>
<div id="lem:spectral-lipschitz" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:spectral-lipschitz"></a>
**Lemma 18**. *For real symmetric matrices $M,N\in\mathbb R^{m\times m}$, $$\label{eq:spectral-lipschitz}
  |q(M)-q(N)|_{\mathrm F}\le |M-N|_{\mathrm F}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz</summary>

*Proof.* Let $(u_i)$ and $(v_k)$ be orthonormal eigenbases of $M$ and $N$, with eigenvalues $\lambda_i$ and $\mu_k$. Then $$u_i^{\mathsf T}(q(M)-q(N))v_k
   =(q(\lambda_i)-q(\mu_k))\,u_i^{\mathsf T}v_k,$$ whereas the corresponding entry of $M-N$ is $(\lambda_i-\mu_k)u_i^{\mathsf T}v_k$. The scalar bound $0<q'<1$ therefore bounds the absolute value of each former entry by the absolute value of the latter. Squaring and summing over $i,k$ proves <a href="#eq:spectral-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spectral-lipschitz">[eq:spectral-lipschitz]</a>, because the Frobenius norm is unchanged by orthogonal changes of the row and column bases separately. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz</summary>

<a id="eq:equality-approximation"></a>
*Proof of the equality statement in Theorem <a href="#intro:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:entropy">2</a>.* Let $f$ be a log-concave probability density on $\mathbb R^m$ with $\delta(f)=0$. Choose the approximating densities $f_j$ from Lemma <a href="#geo:approximation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:approximation">16</a>. Write $a_j,a$ for their means and the mean of $f$, and $C_j,C$ for their covariance matrices. That lemma gives $$\label{eq:equality-approximation}
  \lVert f_j-f\rVert_{L^1(\mathbb R^m)}\longrightarrow0,\qquad
  a_j\longrightarrow a,\qquad C_j\longrightarrow C,\qquad
  \delta(f_j)\longrightarrow0.$$ Here $C$ is positive definite, as recalled in the introduction, so the covariance convergence also implies convergence of the log determinants.

<a id="eq:vanishing-slack"></a>
For each $j$, apply Proposition <a href="#tr:normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tr:normalization">5</a> to $f_j$. Let $P_j$ be its positive definite normalizing matrix and let $F_j$ transport $\gamma_m$ to $(P_j)_\#(f_j(x)\,\,\mathrm dx)$. In the notation of Theorem <a href="#mat:entropy-smooth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:entropy-smooth">[mat:entropy-smooth]</a>, put $$\begin{gathered}
  J_j=DF_j,\qquad A_j=q^{-1}(J_j),\qquad
  X_{j,r}=\mathbb E[x_rA_j(x)],\\
  A_j(x)=\sum_{r=1}^m x_rX_{j,r}+Y_j(x),\qquad
  B_j=\sum_{r=1}^m X_{j,r}^2.
 \end{gathered}$$ The entropy gap is affine invariant. Theorem <a href="#mat:entropy-smooth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="mat:entropy-smooth">[mat:entropy-smooth]</a> and <a href="#eq:equality-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:equality-approximation">[eq:equality-approximation]</a> therefore imply $$\label{eq:vanishing-slack}
  \lVert Y_j\rVert_{L^2(\gamma_m)}\longrightarrow0,
  \qquad
  \sum_{i=1}^m \omega(\lambda_i(B_j))\longrightarrow0.$$ The function $\omega$ is continuous and nonnegative on $[0,\infty)$, vanishes only at $1$, and tends to infinity at infinity. For every $\varepsilon>0$, it consequently has a positive infimum on $\{\lambda\ge0:|\lambda-1|\ge\varepsilon\}$. Thus every eigenvalue of $B_j$ tends to $1$, and $B_j\to I$ in operator norm.

<a id="eq:linear-field-limit"></a>
<a id="eq:jacobian-limit"></a>
Since $\sum_r|X_{j,r}|_{\mathrm F}^2=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B_j$, the coefficients form a bounded sequence in a finite-dimensional space. Pass to a subsequence such that $X_{j,r}\to X_r$ for every $r$. Each $X_r$ is symmetric, and Gaussian orthogonality together with <a href="#eq:vanishing-slack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vanishing-slack">[eq:vanishing-slack]</a> gives $$\label{eq:linear-field-limit}
  \sum_{r=1}^mX_r^2=I,\qquad
  A_j\longrightarrow A(x):=\sum_{r=1}^m x_rX_r
      \quad\text{in }L^2(\gamma_m).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:spectral-lipschitz" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:spectral-lipschitz">18</a> now gives $$\label{eq:jacobian-limit}
  J_j=q(A_j)\longrightarrow J:=q(A)
      \quad\text{in }L^2(\gamma_m).$$

<a id="eq:map-cauchy"></a>
To obtain a limiting map, remove the irrelevant translations: $\widehat F_j=F_j-\mathbb EF_j$. Each $\widehat F_j$ lies in $H^1(\gamma_m;\mathbb R^m)$: its law has finite second moments and, for this fixed $j$, its Jacobian has the bounds <a href="#tr:Jacobian-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="tr:Jacobian-bounds">[tr:Jacobian-bounds]</a> applied to the transformed target. Gaussian Poincaré, applied componentwise to the centered difference, gives $$\label{eq:map-cauchy}
  \lVert \widehat F_j-\widehat F_k\rVert_{L^2(\gamma_m)}^2
    \le \lVert J_j-J_k\rVert_{L^2(\gamma_m)}^2.$$ Consequently $\widehat F_j$ converges strongly in Gaussian $H^1$ to a centered map $\widehat F$ with weak Jacobian $D\widehat F=J$. Indeed, <a href="#eq:map-cauchy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:map-cauchy">[eq:map-cauchy]</a> and <a href="#eq:jacobian-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:jacobian-limit">[eq:jacobian-limit]</a> give convergence of both maps and first derivatives; passage to weak derivatives is also valid on every bounded set, where the Gaussian density has a positive lower bound. In particular, $\widehat F\in W^{1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^m;\mathbb R^m)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:linear-jacobian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:linear-jacobian">17</a> applies to <a href="#eq:linear-field-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:linear-field-limit">[eq:linear-field-limit]</a> and shows that $\widehat F_\#\gamma_m$ is an affine exponential product with covariance $I$.

<a id="eq:normalization-compactness"></a>
It remains to return from the normalized laws to $f$. By Cauchy–Schwarz, the strong $L^2(\gamma_m)$ convergence of the centered maps gives <a id="eq:normalized-covariance-limit"></a>$$\label{eq:normalized-covariance-limit}
  D_j:=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}((\widehat F_j)_\#\gamma_m)
      =P_jC_jP_j^{\mathsf T}\longrightarrow I.$$ We can now bound the normalizing matrices uniformly, without any uniform Hessian bounds on the approximating potentials. Choose positive constants $c,C_0,d,D$ such that, for all sufficiently large $j$, $$cI\le C_j\le C_0I,\qquad dI\le D_j\le DI.$$ Congruence by $P_j$ then yields $$\label{eq:normalization-compactness}
  \frac d{C_0}I\le P_jP_j^{\mathsf T}\le\frac Dc I.$$ Hence $P_j$ and $P_j^{-1}$ are bounded. Pass to a further subsequence with $P_j\to P$; the lower bound in <a href="#eq:normalization-compactness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalization-compactness">[eq:normalization-compactness]</a> makes $P$ invertible.

The law of $\widehat F_j$ is the image of $f_j(x)\,\,\mathrm dx$ under $x\mapsto P_j(x-a_j)$. For a bounded continuous function $\psi$, $$\begin{aligned}
 &\left|\int\psi(P_j(x-a_j))f_j(x)\,\,\mathrm dx
       -\int\psi(P(x-a))f(x)\,\,\mathrm dx\right|\\
 &\quad\le\lVert \psi\rVert_{\infty}\lVert f_j-f\rVert_{L^1}
     +\int\left|\psi(P_j(x-a_j))-\psi(P(x-a))\right|f(x)\,\,\mathrm dx
     \longrightarrow0.
\end{aligned}$$ The first term tends to zero by <a href="#eq:equality-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:equality-approximation">[eq:equality-approximation]</a>; the second does so by dominated convergence. Strong convergence of $\widehat F_j$ also identifies the weak limit of these laws as $\widehat F_\#\gamma_m$. It follows that the image of $f(x)\,\,\mathrm dx$ under $x\mapsto P(x-a)$ is the affine exponential product already determined above. Since $P$ is invertible, $f$ itself has the form asserted in Theorem <a href="#intro:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:entropy">2</a>, up to equality almost everywhere.

Conversely, the density of a product of $m$ independent mean-one exponentials is log-concave, has entropy $m$, and has covariance $I$. Every invertible affine image remains log-concave and has the same entropy gap by affine invariance. Thus every density in the stated class satisfies $\delta=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:geometry"></a>
### The simplex inequality and its equality cases

We transfer Theorem <a href="#intro:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:entropy">2</a> to convex bodies using the exponential density on a cone. This construction appears in (Fradelizi and Meyer 2008, Proposition 2(b)) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[FradeliziMeyer2008](#ref-FradeliziMeyer2008)]; its entropy reduction is (Fradelizi and Marín Sola 2026, Lemma 5.2 and Proposition 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[FradeliziMarinSola2026](#ref-FradeliziMarinSola2026)]. The moment formulas also appear in (Bo’az Klartag 2018, Lemma 2.5) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Klartag2018](#ref-Klartag2018)]. The calculation identifies equality in the body inequality with entropy equality in one higher dimension. The support of the resulting exponential product then determines the body.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-spectral-lipschitz</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#intro:simplex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:simplex">1</a>.* Let $K\subset\mathbb R^n$ be a convex body. Translation preserves both $L_K$ and the property of being a simplex, so assume that $K$ has centroid zero. Write $\Sigma_K=\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(X)$ for $X$ uniform on $K$, and define <a id="eq:body-cone"></a>$$\label{eq:body-cone}
  \mathcal C_K=\{(tx,t):x\in K,\ t\geq0\}\subset\mathbb R^{n+1}.$$ Convexity of $K$ makes $\mathcal C_K$ convex. It is closed because $K$ is compact: a convergent sequence $(t_jx_j,t_j)$ with $t_j\to0$ has $t_jx_j\to0$, and when the limiting height is positive one can divide by that height. The cone has nonempty interior, and its only point at height zero is the origin.

<a id="eq:body-cone-bound"></a>
Define the log-concave density <a id="eq:body-cone-density"></a>$$\label{eq:body-cone-density}
  f_K(y,t)=\frac{e^{-t}}{n!\,|K|}\,
  \mathbf 1_{\mathcal C_K}(y,t).$$ The change of variables $y=tx$, with Jacobian $t^n$ for $t>0$, gives $$\int_{\mathcal C_K}e^{-t}\,\,\mathrm dy\,\,\mathrm dt
  =|K|\int_0^\infty t^ne^{-t}\,\,\mathrm dt=n!\,|K|,$$ so $f_K$ is a probability density. The same change of variables shows that it is the density of $(TX,T)$, where $T$ is independent of $X$ and has density $t^ne^{-t}/n!$ on $(0,\infty)$. Integration by parts therefore gives $$\mathbb ET=n+1,\qquad
  \mathbb ET^2=(n+1)(n+2),\qquad
  \mathop{\mathrm{Var}}(T)=n+1.$$ Since $\mathbb EX=0$, independence makes the cross-covariance of $TX$ and $T$ vanish. Hence <a id="eq:body-cone-entropy"></a><a id="eq:body-cone-covariance"></a><a id="eq:body-cone-determinant"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
  h(f_K)&=n+1+\log(n!\,|K|),\label{eq:body-cone-entropy}\\
  \mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f_K)&=
  \begin{pmatrix}
    (n+1)(n+2)\Sigma_K&0\\
    0&n+1
  \end{pmatrix},\label{eq:body-cone-covariance}\\
  \det\mathop{\mathrm{Cov}}(f_K)&=(n+1)^{n+1}(n+2)^n\det\Sigma_K.
  \label{eq:body-cone-determinant}
\end{aligned}$$ Applying the entropy inequality of Theorem <a href="#intro:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:entropy">2</a> in dimension $n+1$ yields $$\label{eq:body-cone-bound}
  (n!)^2|K|^2\geq
  (n+1)^{n+1}(n+2)^n\det\Sigma_K.$$ This is precisely $$L_K\leq
  \frac{(n!)^{1/n}}{(n+1)^{(n+1)/(2n)}\sqrt{n+2}}.$$ Every step from the entropy inequality to <a href="#eq:body-cone-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:body-cone-bound">[eq:body-cone-bound]</a> is reversible. Thus equality in this bound holds if and only if $f_K$ has equality in Theorem <a href="#intro:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:entropy">2</a>.

<a id="eq:body-cone-affine-orthant"></a>
Suppose equality holds. By that theorem, the law with density $f_K$ is an invertible affine image of the product of $n+1$ mean-one exponential laws. The closed support of $f_K$ is exactly $\mathcal C_K$: it has positive density on the interior of the cone, and this interior is dense in the cone. Taking closed supports gives $$\label{eq:body-cone-affine-orthant}
  \mathcal C_K=b+M[0,\infty)^{n+1}$$ for an invertible matrix $M$ and a vector $b$.

The origin is the unique extreme point of $\mathcal C_K$. Indeed, every nonzero $z\in\mathcal C_K$ is the midpoint of $0$ and $2z$, whereas $0=(u+v)/2$ with $u,v\in\mathcal C_K$ forces the nonnegative heights of $u$ and $v$ to vanish, and hence $u=v=0$. The orthant also has the origin as its unique extreme point. Invertible affine maps preserve extreme points, so <a href="#eq:body-cone-affine-orthant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:body-cone-affine-orthant">[eq:body-cone-affine-orthant]</a> forces $b=0$.

<a id="eq:body-cone-section"></a>
Choose positive multiples $w_0,\ldots,w_n$ of the respective columns of $M$, and let $a_i$ be the last coordinate of $w_i$. Each $w_i$ is a nonzero point of $\mathcal C_K$, so $a_i>0$. Put $p_i=w_i/a_i$. The section at height one is $$\label{eq:body-cone-section}
  K\times\{1\}
  =\left\{\sum_{i=0}^n c_iw_i:
      c_i\geq0,\ \sum_{i=0}^n c_i a_i=1\right\}
  =\operatorname{conv}\{p_0,\ldots,p_n\}.$$ The vectors $p_i$ are linearly independent, since the vectors $w_i$ are. They are consequently affinely independent in the height-one hyperplane. Thus $K$ is a simplex. Figure <a href="#fig:cone-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:cone-section">1</a> illustrates the normalization of the generating rays in this step.

Conversely, let $K=\operatorname{conv}\{v_0,\ldots,v_n\}$ be a simplex, and let $M$ have columns $(v_i,1)$. These columns are linearly independent and $$M[0,\infty)^{n+1}=\mathcal C_K,
  \qquad |\det M|=n!\,|K|.$$ For the determinant identity, subtract the first column from the others and expand along the last row; the remaining determinant is $n!$ times the volume of $K$, up to sign. If $E_0,\ldots,E_n$ are independent mean-one exponential variables, the density of $M(E_0,\ldots,E_n)^{\mathsf T}$ at $(y,t)\in\mathcal C_K$ is $$\frac{1}{|\det M|}\exp\left(-\sum_{i=0}^n
    (M^{-1}(y,t))_i\right)
  =\frac{e^{-t}}{n!\,|K|},$$ because the last row of $M$ consists of ones. This is exactly $f_K$, up to immaterial boundary values. Theorem <a href="#intro:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:entropy">2</a> therefore gives equality in <a href="#eq:body-cone-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:body-cone-bound">[eq:body-cone-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

For $n=1$, every convex body is an interval, hence a simplex, and the constant is $1/\sqrt{12}$. Indeed, an interval of length $\ell$ has variance $\ell^2/12$, in agreement with the formula above.

<figure id="fig:cone-section">

<figcaption>The height-one section of a cone generated by three independent rays. Dividing each generator $w_{i}$ by its positive height $a_{i}$ produces the vertices $p_{i}$ of the section; one generator above the section is shown explicitly. Formula <a href="#eq:body-cone-section" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:body-cone-section">[eq:body-cone-section]</a> gives the same correspondence in every dimension.</figcaption>
</figure>
